Field Theory via Higher Geometry II: Thickened Smooth Sets as Synthetic Foundations
Grigorios Giotopoulos, Hisham Sati

Abstract
This is the second in a series of papers that aim to develop rigorous and most encompassing foundations for field theory, where in the first installment [ Part I ] we laid out the natural formulation of bosonic variational field theory via the “functorial geometry” of smooth sets, namely in the topos over the site of spaces with smooth maps between them. Here, we extend this to the category ThSmthSet \mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet} of infinitesimally thickened smooth sets . We first describe the Cahiers topos in a simplified, but fully rigorous, ℝ \mathbbm{R} -algebraic setting – which should serve as a more accessible introduction to the theory of Synthetic Differential Geometry to both physicists and mathematicians. Then, we formulate local Lagrangian field theory in this rigorous setting in which infinitesimal spaces exist and interact correctly with the field-theoretic spaces of infinite jet bundles, off-shell and on-shell spaces of fields etc. This setting subsumes all previous constructions (see [ Part I ] ) and further recovers all the relevant tangent bundles of traditional (off-shell and on-shell) field theory considerations via the synthetic tangent bundle construction, i.e., as “infinitesimal curves” in those spaces, which were previously defined only in an ad-hoc manner. Beyond finally putting such aspects of the theory on a firm foundation, this approach recognizes the variational principle of local Lagrangian field theory, equivalently, as an intersection of thickened smooth sets. It also suggests the rigorous formalization of perturbative field theory as the (literal) restriction to a (synthetic) infinitesimal neighborhood 𝔻 ϕ ↪ ℱ \mathbbm{D}_{\phi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F} around a field configuration. Furthermore, our context naturally accomodates more general rigorous considerations, where the manifolds may have boundaries and corners, a situation which has been recently attracting greater attention in the field-theoretical literature.

1
Introduction

This is the second in a series of papers that aim to lay rigorous foundations for field theories.
In the first installment
[
Part I
]
we set up a natural formulation of variational field theory via the “functorial geometry” of
smooth sets
, namely in the topos over the site of Cartesian spaces with smooth maps between them.
Here, we further refine this formulation to thickened smooth sets, which are in addition equipped with infinitesimal extension.
We show, by a list of constructive results and examples, that this is an even more natural setting
to accommodate further intuitive notions used in (local) classical field theory. More explicitly, this forms a well-adapted topos
for “
Synthetic Differential Geometry
” (SDG), defined as sheaves over a ‘thickened’ version of Cartesian spaces that includes test probes with ‘infinitesimal directions’, also known as the
Cahiers topos
.
That is, we consider a category of smooth geometrical objects that are furthermore probe-able by certain well-defined infinitesimal spaces.
In other words, these “infinitesimally thickened smooth sets” will have, in addition to a smooth structure, a well-defined notion of infinitesimal structure, both essential for a proper treatment of field theory.
Schematically,

Smooth structure
+

Infinitesimal structure

\leadsto
Cahiers topos

hence containing objects of finite (smooth) and infinitesimal extent, e.g.,

Thickened
point,
\mathbb{D}

Thickened
\mathbb{R}

The setting.

In
[
Part I
]
, we used the category
\mathrm{CartSp}
as probes, Cartesian spaces with smooth maps between them,
which yielded the same notion of generalized smooth spaces (smooth sets) as probing with the whole category
\mathrm{SmthMfd}
of smooth manifolds instead.
A most useful fact towards further generalized notions of geometrical spaces is to work dually with the corresponding algebras of smooth functions, captured in
the (opposite) category of commutative algebras
\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}
, giving a fully faithful
composite
\mathrm{CartSp}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{SmthMfd}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}
. Indeed, this allows to enlarge our original site of probe spaces so as to include infinitesimal points and their products with Cartesian spaces, leading to the category of (infinitesimally) thickened Cartesian spaces

\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
. We intuitively think of objects, denoted by
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
,
as the usual Cartesian spaces, supplied with an extra ‘halo of infinitesimal directions’ around each point
x\in\mathbbm{R}^{k}
. This category becomes a site by defining the (Grothendieck) coverage to be that of (differentiably) good open covers,
extended trivially along infinitesimal directions.
Considering maps between these objects as formally dual to ‘pullbacks’ between their function algebras in CAlg
R
, leads
as above to the fully faithful composite

\mathrm{ThCartSp}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}
,
where
\mathrm{ThCartSp}
is the thickened version of
\mathrm{CartSp}
, and similarly
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}
of
\mathrm{SmthMfd}
. Overall, we have the following diagram of fully faithful inclusions between sites of probes, along with corresponding projections that forget the infinitesimal extensions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CartSp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SmthMfd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}}

We will show that the category of spaces probe-able by such thickened smooth probes is the proper rigorous (and intuitive) setting to naturally define the notions of tangent vectors in all sorts of field theoretic spaces: manifolds with boundaries and corners, infinite jet bundles, off-shell and on-shell field spaces. These are all constructed in such a way that immediately recovers the objects introduced in
[
Part I
]
and hence in turn those already used in the literature. differential. Moreover, this setting allows us to rigorously formalize the intuition of “infinitesimal neighborhoods”, hence allowing to view jets of fields as sections over infinitesimal neighborhoods of points, and initial conditions as sections over infinitesimal neighborhoods of codimension-1 submanifolds of spacetime.

Literature 1.1

(
Synthetic differential geometry: foundations).

The idea of axiomatizing differential geometry using inspiration from topos theory
goes back to Lawvere
[
La67
]
(see also a modernized perspective in
[
La98
]
).
Further developments, especially in terms of refining the model-building
were originally provided by Dubuc
[
Du79
]
in terms of sheaf toposes on the category of smooth loci, certain formal duals of
C^{\infty}
-algebras (more commonly known as
C^{\infty}
-rings).
Detailed discussion on infinitesimals is in
[
Pe85
]
.
The topic has been taken up within differentially cohesive homotopy type theory

[
We17
]
[
JM22
]
[
Ch24
]
.
Surveys in the form of books include an overview of geometric structures in
[
Lav96
]
,
calculations and applications in
[
Be98
]
,
axiomatics in
[
Ko06
]
[
Ko10
]
, and further concrete constructions of well-adapted models using the technology of
C^{\infty}
-algebras in
[
BD86
]
[
MR91
]
. Shorter expositions include

[
Re86
]
[
Ko16
]
[
Shu06
]
[
Be00
]
[
Kos09
]
[
Sch23
]
.
See also
[
BGS18
]
for further extension to differential topology.

Literature 1.2

(
Synthetic differential geometry: applications).

For the original motivation of SDG with an aim to applications to motion and continuum mechanics, see
[
La80
]
[
La97
]
[
La02
]
.
A framework in which Ehresmann’s theory of jets can be treated from the viewpoint of synthetic differential geometry, i.e., in the categorical study of a well-adapted topos is in
[
Ko80
]
.
An alternative attempt at a formulation of jet bundles is given in
[
Ni01
]
.
Lie’s geometric theory of first-order differential equations is treated in
[
Ko25
]
. A modern perspective of PDE theory in the Cahiers topos is provided in
[
KS17
]
. Synthetic reasoning has been used to provide a derivation of Einstein’s equation for general relativity in

[
Re11
]
.
Poisson algebras for non-linear scalar field theories are treated in
[
BS17
]
, and our treatment here
can be viewed as a vast generalization of their observation and for all Lagrangian field theories
in full detail.

Our approach.
We highlight the following:

•

We are not working in the setting of synthetic differential geometry in the axiomatic
sense, but rather
externally
via a particularly approachable and utilizable sheaf topos model, namely the Cahiers topos of Dubuc, in our simpler and more down-to-earth incarnation of it as infinitesimally thickened smooth sets. In other words, we will often be phrasing our discussion in a
plot-wise
manner (also known as level-wise or object-wise), following the intuition arising from physical considerations.

•

We carefully show that the original Cahiers topos of Dubuc is, in fact,
\mathbbm{R}
-algebraic in disguise. Indeed, the perhaps abstract and technically heavier machinery of
C^{\infty}
-algebras might have been an obstacle in the dissemination of the ideas of (and models of) synthetic differential geometry; we hope this result should make the subject more accessible, by guaranteeing that one may safely bypass familiarity with the notions of
C^{\infty}
-algebras (at least at the beginner’s level).

•

Generally, our approach highlights a streamlined presentation with
pedagogy in mind to the extent possible.
Our main goal here is to lay out a foundation that is both mathematically sound and practical, convenient, and staying
close to the established practice/intuition of constructions using “infinitesimals” in physics.

While it is our goal to develop Lagrangian field theory in the above rigorous sense, we highlight
some of the previous, several being more traditional, approaches:

.
Another approach close to ours as a rigorous formalization of (bosonic) field theories

[
Bl24b
]
.

Also for this topic, extensive literature is cited in
[
Part I
]
, while here we highlighted the most relevant ones,
which include the cohomological properties within various
approaches in

[

.
A detailed exposition is given in

.

Structure and outline of results.

•

In
section

2
, we motivate and develop in detail infinitesimally thickened smooth sets as the main backdrop for
where bosonic field theories ought to be defined.

–

In
section

2.1
, we consider infinitesimal probes living alongside Cartesian spaces,
by recalling how Cartesian spaces and smooth manifolds embed into commutative algebras (Prop.
2.1
). We then
describe tangent vectors on smooth manifolds via infinitesimals (Lem.
2.2
),
define infinitesimal disks and more generally infinitesimally thickened points,
and show how the latter are subspaces of the former (Lem.
2.8
).

–

In
section

2.2
, we consider sheaves over infinitesimally thickened smooth probes.
We enlarge our original site of probe spaces CartSp from
[
Part I
]
within the (opposite) category
of commutative algebras, so as to include infinitesimally thickened points and their products with Cartesian spaces (hence thickened
versions of Cartesian spaces). We demonstrate how thickened manifolds, manifolds with corners, and smooth closed subspaces,
are naturally examples of thickened smooth sets. We also describe the thickened moduli space of de Rham forms.

–

In
section

2.3
,
we define thickened smooth mapping spaces and thickened smooth sets of sections, allowing us to consider the “synthetic tangent bundle” of a manifold (Prop.
2.22
).
We then consider the synthetic tangent bundle of infinitesimal disks and manifold mapping spaces (Lem.
2.27
), as well as that of field spaces of sections (Prop.
2.28
).
We show how to represent plots of tangent vectors via paths of plots (Lem.
2.29
),
define the variational cotangent bundle, and exhibit the intuitively desired property of tangent vectors within thickened smooth sets, namely that path derivations depend only on tangent vectors (Lem.
2.32
).
We define thickened infinite jet bundles, recover their tangent bundles synthetically (Lem.
2.34
),
and then describe how to explicit represent plots of
J^{\infty}_{M}F
and its tangent bundle.

–

In
section

2.4
, we consider the natural relationship of infinitesimal neighborhoods and jets,
defining them for manifolds, and describing jets as sections over those
(Lem.
2.37
). We define the infinitesimal shape
and synthetic infinitesimal neighborhoods of arbitrary thickened smooth sets (Def.
2.39
), and show how the latter recovers the more traditional notion for manifolds
(Lem.
2.40
). We motivate how the restriction of local field theoretic functionals to these neighborhoods in field spaces ought to encode the traditional practice of perturbative field theory, by showing this explicitly in the case of field theory over a point-spacetime.
We describe
\infty
-jets of sections as sections over infinitesimal neighborhoods and define synthetic infinitesimal neighborhoods of arbitrary submanifolds. We then describe jets over submanifolds as sections over infinitesimal neighborhoods of submanifolds (Prop.
2.47
), hence making rigorous the intuition of initial conditions as fields defined over infinitesimal neighborhoods of (codimension 1) submanifolds.

•

In
section

3
, we undertake our description of local Lagrangian field theory in the above setting.

–

In
section

3.1
, we study the geometry of the infinite jet bundle,
considering horizontal and vertical splittings, characterizing the vertical jet bundle
(Lem.
3.1
), the splitting of the infinite jet tangent bundle
(Prop.
3.2
), and the synthetic pushfoward of jet prolongated section
(Cor.
3.3
).
Then, we describe how the variational bicomplex arises
completely within thickened smooth sets, along with the corresponding results on vertical and
horizontal cohomologies.

–

This leads us naturally, in
section

3.2
, to consider the transgression and the local bicomplex.
We describe a direct extension to ThSmthSet for all the definitions and related results regarding local Lagrangians and local currents/functionals.
For this, we describe the crucial ingredient of the thickened infinite jet prolongation (Lem.
3.8
), which
as a concrete example
we apply explicitly to the Euler-Lagrange operator.
We then describe the synthetic tangent bundle of jet bundle sections (Lem.
3.10
)
and the synthetic pushforward of jet prolongation (Prop.
3.11
). This leads us
to consider the pushforward of the evaluation map of sections
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{F}^{\infty}\times M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
of the infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow M
(Prop.
3.12
), together with an explicit description of the pushforward of corresponding prolongated evaluation
(Cor.
3.13
).
We end by showing how the bicomplex of local forms and its Cartan calculus thus appear naturally within ThSmthSet.

–

In
section

3.3
, we describe on-shell fields as a thickened smooth critical set.
We define critical plots of a thickened smooth map and establish
functoriality of the thickened critical set (Thm.
3.17
) for local field theories, by exhibiting its equivalence with the Euler–Lagrange locus.
This then naturally yields the identification of the synthetic tangent bundle of on-shell fields as the space of Jacobi fields
(Cor.
3.20
).

•

Our Appendix
section

4

serves as a technical, purely mathematical backbone, and could be of independent interest to
those seeking deeper perspectives on synthetic differential geometry.

–

Firstly, in
section

4.1
, we justify our usage of the
\mathbbm{R}
-algebraic version of Dubuc’s Cahiers topos comprised of sheaves over infinitesimally thickened Cartesian probes
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}^{\mathrm{op}}
, rather than the original
C^{\infty}
-algebraic version from
[
Du79
]
, via Thm.
4.7
and Cor.
4.8
.
We prove a generalized version of Hadamard’s lemma for partial derivatives (Lem.
4.1
) and extend to manifolds
and Cartesian products (Cor.
4.2
), which imply that the function algebras of our thickened probes are all quotients of smooth
Cartesian algebras (Cor.
4.3
).
We establish that
\mathbb{R}
-algebras map into thickened Cartesian algebras are automatically
C^{\infty}
-algebraic (Prop.
4.6
), which implies that our
thickened Cartesian spaces qualify as smooth loci (Thm.
4.7
). The latter immediately implies that the Cahiers topos is
\mathbbm{R}
-algebraic (Cor.
4.8
), being equivalent to our
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
.

–

Secondly, in
section

4.2
,
we show how manifolds with boundaries and corners embed both into (plain) commutative
\mathbbm{R}
-algebras, and also in our
\mathbbm{R}
-algebraic version of the Cahiers topos, i.e., thickened smooth sets. We do this also for the theory of Weil bundles over them, by first
including a concise review of traditional Weil bundles, in a manner directly applicable to manifolds with corners. Concretely, we extend Milnor’s exercise to the case of smooth closed subspaces
(Cor.
4.11
) and further extend to the desirable setting of manifolds with corners
(Cor.
4.12
). We use the latter to show how smooth manifolds with boundaries and corners embed into (plain) commutative
\mathbbm{R}
-algebras
(Prop.
4.13
).
We then treat Weil bundles of manifolds with corners in
Prop.
4.19
and show how these are recovered via the
synthetic Weil bundle construction in
Prop.
4.20
by working internally to
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
.

–

Thirdly, in
section

4.3
, we define the infinitesimal neighborhood along
a morphism (Lem.
4.21
) of arbitrary thickened smooth sets,
and use it to define the
synthetic jet bundle of any such map, which we prove is a thickened smooth set (Lem.
4.25
).
We establish the adjunction between the diagonal neighborhood functor
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{F}}
and the synthetic
\infty
-jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{\mathcal{F}}
functor
(Prop.
4.29
).
We then define the synthetic jet prolongation,
and show how this recovers the traditional infinite jet prolongation in the case of fiber bundles of manifolds (Lem.
4.32
).

–

Lastly, in
section

4.4
, we
define the fully classifying version of the moduli space of de Rham forms.
Our approach here
allows for a direct extraction of its classifying property, which has remained widely underappreciated due to its somewhat obscure realization in the original axiomatic SDG approaches.
For instance, it is immediate in our setting that the moduli space of 1-forms
\widehat{\mathbold{\Omega}}^{1}\in\mathcal{E}
classifies a subset of
smooth maps
T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
out of the synthetic tangent bundle, which identifies the former as a linear subobject
(Cor.
4.35
) of a larger classifier of all (non-linear) functions out of tangent bundles.
The full (linear) classifying nature of form moduli is established in Prop.
4.36
.
The algebraic moduli space is shown to naturally be a subspace of the fully classifying moduli space
(Lem.
4.38
) and they become isomorphic in a larger topos where tangent bundles are
representable (Cor.
4.42
).
We finally briefly describe how the above extends to differential
n
-form classifying spaces.

2
Infinitesimally thickened smooth sets

Recall that in
[
Part I
]
we presented the natural formulation of variational field theory via the “functorial geometry” of
smooth sets
, namely in the topos over the site of Cartesian spaces with smooth maps between them.
In this section, we further refine the latter topos to a setting for “synthetic differential geometry” via
thickened smooth sets
, which are, in addition, equipped with infinitesimal extension.

2.1
Infinitesimally thickened probes

Our first goal is to identify a category of
infinitesimal probe spaces

\mathbbm{D}
, such that generalized spaces
\mathcal{F}
may be defined as being probe-able by them, in such a manner that
\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{D})
may be reasonably interpreted as the
infinitesimal plots
in
\mathcal{F}
. This is in direct analogy to
\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
being interpreted as the set of smooth
k
-dimensional plots of finite (or infinite) extent in
\mathcal{F}
.

None of the Cartesian probe spaces in our original site
\mathrm{CartSp}
serve such a purpose,
and so we must enlarge the site by introducing infinitesimal probe spaces. To that end, we recall the following crucial but possibly underappreciated result, which follows by “Milnor’s exercise” (Prop.
4.10
, following e.g.
[
KMS93
, §35.9]
). A (generalized) proof of the following is included in the Appendix (Prop.
4.13
), following the standard case (e.g.
[
KMS93
, §35.10]
).

Proposition 2.1

(
Smooth manifolds embed into Algebras).

The functor

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(-)\;:\;\mathrm{SmthMfd}

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;C^{\infty}(M)\,,

sending a finite-dimensional smooth (second countable and Hausdorff) manifold to its function algebra is fully faithful, in that for any pair
N,M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
the smooth functions
f:N\xrightarrow{\;}M
biject onto the algebra homomorphisms
f^{\ast}:C^{\infty}(M)\xrightarrow{\;}C^{\infty}(N)
.
Hence also the composite

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(-)\,:\,\mathrm{CartSp}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathrm{SmthMfd}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}

(1)

is fully faithful.

In the spirit of algebraic geometry, it follows that the objects of
\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}
may be thought of as spaces generalizing smooth manifolds, being formally dual to some algebra of functions. Indeed, among these generalized smooth spaces we may find smooth manifolds with
boundaries and corners
(Prop.
4.13
), infinitesimal spaces, and furthermore smooth manifolds equipped with
infinitesimal thickenings
.
The archetypical example of an infinitesimal space (e.g.
[
Mu88
, p. 218]
) is the one dual to the “algebra of dual numbers” (
[
Ko81
, pp. xi, 4]
[
MR91
, p. 19]
following
[
Gr09
]
)

\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)\;:=\;\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}\,,

which may be thought of as the smooth functions on the first order infinitesimal line (or neighborhood)
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}
of the origin
0\in\mathbbm{R}
in the real line. One thinks of
\epsilon\in\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)
as the linear coordinate function on
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
,
which squares to zero since ‘all points are infinitesimally close to the origin’. Such a quotient rigorously captures the idea of dropping higher-order terms of infinitesimally small quantities,
as often informally practiced in the physics literature.
Following the formal dual intuition, the algebra projection map
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)
, given by the first-order
Taylor expansion
f\mapstochar\rightarrow f(0)+\epsilon\cdot f^{\prime}(0)
, may be thought of as dually encoding the
‘embedding’

\iota_{0}\;:\;\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathbbm{R}^{1}\,,

of this infinitesimal neighborhood into the real line. The suggestive naming for
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
is justified by the following classical result (e.g.
[
Ko81
, §I.7]
).

Lemma 2.2

(
Tangent vectors via infinitesimals).

For any smooth manifold
M
, potentially with boundary and corners
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}
, there is a canonical bijection

\displaystyle TM\;\cong_{\mathrm{Set}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(C^{\infty}(M),\mathcal{O}\big(D^{1}(1)\big)\!\Big)

(2)

of tangent vectors on
M
with (formally dual) maps of
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
into
M
.

Proof.

An algebra homomorphism
C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}

has components

\big(p,X_{p}\big)\;:\;f\;\longmapsto\;p(f)+\epsilon\cdot X_{p}(f)\,.

Using the homomorphism property and
\epsilon^{2}=0
, it follows for
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(M)
that

(f_{1}\cdot f_{2})\;\longmapsto\;p(f_{1})\cdot p(f_{2})+\epsilon\big(p(f_{1})\cdot X_{p}(f_{2})+X_{p}(f_{1})\cdot p(f_{2})\big)\,

and therefore

•

the first component is an algebra homomorphism
p:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}\cong C^{\infty}(*)
, and hence necessarily coincides with the evaluation at a point
p\in M
(Prop.
4.10
, Cor.
4.12
);

•

the second component is a derivation
X_{p}:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
at
p\in M

1
1

1

More precisely, a derivation of the germs of functions
C^{\infty}_{p}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
at the point
p\in M
.
, i.e., a tangent vector at
p\in M
.∎

The intuitive interpretation of this result is that a (formal dual) map

\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}M\,,

is an (first order) infinitesimal curve segment in
M
, or said otherwise, an (first order) infinitesimal line plot.

Remark 2.3

(
Infinitesimal vs finite curves).

The above, almost trivial, example already hints at the power of considering infinitesimal spaces as bona fide geometrical objects.

(i)

Indeed, recall that for manifolds without boundary or corners, tangent vectors are more often introduced as
equivalency classes
2
2

2

Two curves with
\gamma_{1}(0)=x=\gamma_{2}(0)
are considered as equivalent
\gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}
if
\partial_{t}(f\circ\gamma_{1})|_{t}=\partial_{t}(f\circ\gamma_{2})|_{t=0}
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
of smooth curves

\gamma\,:\,\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M\,.

While this is an equivalent characterization for manifolds without boundary or corners, which captures the ‘infinitesimal part’ of the curves via the imposed equivalency relation, this is no longer true when considering manifolds with boundary and corners. Indeed, it is easy to see that in the latter cases, this approach only recovers part of the full tangent bundle.

(ii)

For instance, consider the case of the upper half plane

\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}_{x,y}:=\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{x}\times[0,\infty)_{y}
, the model space for 2-dimensional manifolds with boundary. By Lem.
2.2
, it follows that the set of infinitesimal curves
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}_{x,y}
recovers the full tangent bundle

T\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}\,\cong\,\mathop{\text{\smash{\raisebox{-9.03763pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\prod$}}}\vphantom{$\prod$}}}_{(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}}\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbbm{R}}\Big\{\partial_{x}|_{(x_{0},y_{0})},\,\partial_{y}|_{(x_{0},y_{0})}\Big\}\,.

Notice, this identification includes outward-pointing tangent vectors at the boundary, corresponding to derivations of the form
-c\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial_{y}}|_{(x_{0},0)}
where
c\in\mathbbm{R}_{>0}
. However, this part of the tangent bundle cannot be represented by (equivalency classes) of smooth curves
3
3

3

Strictly speaking, one has consider
bounded
curves
\gamma_{t}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow M
in manifolds with boundaries/corners to define tangent vectors passing through boundary and corner points
\gamma_{t}(0)\in M
.

\gamma:\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
, since there is no smooth curve inside
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
pointing outwards at the boundary.

(iii)

This phenomenon persists when considering the appropriate notion of tangent vectors (and bundles) over more complicated spaces, such as intersections of manifolds, infinite-dimensional field theoretic spaces, or even the Euler–Lagrange locus of a classical field theory. As we shall see, the tangent bundles of the aforementioned are all correctly identified as maps out of the infinitesimal line, in an appropriate sense (Def.
2.24
), whereas (equivalence classes) of curves might miss several components of their tangent bundles (cf.
[
Part I
, Rem. 7.14]
).

Proceeding analogously, we may define
m
-dimensional disks of
l
-infinitesimal order
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
, for any
m,l\in\mathbbm{N}
,
and consider the corresponding (formally dual) maps
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\rightarrow M
:

Definition 2.4

(
Infinitesimal disks).

For
m,l\in\mathbbm{N}
, the
infinitesimal m-disk of order l
, denoted by
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
, is defined as the formal dual
of

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\,:=\,\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m}]/(\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m})^{l+1}\quad\in\quad\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}\,.

(3)

Just as with the infinitesimal line
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
, every infinitesimal disk
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
also ‘embeds’ into the corresponding
m
-dimensional Cartesian space
\mathbbm{R}^{m}
, around its origin,

\displaystyle\iota_{0}\,:\,\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathbbm{R}^{m}\,,

(4)

in the formal dual sense of the algebra projection map

C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

defined by the
l
-order Taylor expansion

f\;\;\longmapsto\;\;f(0)+\epsilon^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}f(0)\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon^{i}\epsilon^{j}\cdot\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f(0)\;+\;\cdots\;+\;\tfrac{1}{l!}\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{l}=1}^{m}\epsilon^{i_{1}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l}}\cdot\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{l}}f(0)\,.

Remark 2.5

(
On nomenclature of thickened points).

In algebro-geometric terms, one would say

\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\;:=\;\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m}]/(\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m})^{l+1}\big)

is the
spectrum
of
\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m}]/(\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m})^{l+1}
, i.e., the (locally) ringed space

\Big(*\,,\,\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m}]/(\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m})^{l+1}\Big)

over a single point. From this viewpoint, every infinitesimal disk
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
has one actual geometrical point,
and hence one may also refer to it
as an example of an
infinitesimally thickened point
(cf. the more general case of Def.
2.7
).

The relationship of infinitesimal disks and Cartesian spaces is, in fact, tighter. To witness this, we recall a standard result in real analysis. A proof of (a slightly generalized version of) the following is included in the Appendix (Lem.
4.1
).

Lemma 2.6

(
Hadamard’s Lemma).

For any smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})
and any
l\in\mathbbm{N}
, there exist smooth functions
\{h_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l+1}}\}_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{l+1}=1,\cdots,m}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})
such that

\displaystyle f=f(0)+x^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}f(0)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}x^{i}x^{j}\cdot\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f(0)+\cdots+\;\tfrac{1}{l!}\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{l}=1}^{m}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{l}}\cdot\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{l}}f(0)\;+\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{l+1}=1}^{m}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{l+1}}\cdot h_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l+1}}\,,

where
\{x^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})
are the canonical (linear) coordinate functions.

It follows that the function algebra on the infinitesimal disk
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
(Def.
2.4
) is
canonically
identified as the quotient

\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\;\;\cong\;\;C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})/(x^{1},\cdots,x^{m})^{l+1}

(5)

of smooth functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{m}
by (the ideal generated by)
(l+1)
-fold products of the linear coordinate functions. It is in this sense that the embedding of an infinitesimal disk at the origin of the corresponding Cartesian space (
4
) is
canonical
, factoring (dually) through

C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})/(x^{1},\cdots,x^{m})^{l+1}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\,.

We will see that mapping
appropriately out of these infinitesimal spaces detects higher-order variants of tangent vectors (Rem.
2.23
) and jets of sections of fiber bundles (Prop.
2.37
).

More generally, then, one may reasonably argue that (formal duals of) further quotients of the above nilpotent algebras

\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\,/\,I

should also be considered as valid infinitesimal spaces, since the latter algebras are also nilpotent in nature. The most general such consideration along these lines would be to consider any abstract, finite-dimensional and nilpotent algebra
W
as a function algebra on an infinitesimal point
\mathbbm{D}
. Roughly speaking, such an infinitesimal point
\mathbbm{D}
should be any space with finitely many “infinitesimal coordinates”.

Definition 2.7

(
Infinitesimally thickened points).

An infinitesimally thickened point, denoted by
\mathbbm{D}
, is defined as the formal dual of a
“Weil algebra”
(also known as a
“local Artinian algebra”
). More explicitly, it is a space with function algebra of the form

\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\;\cong_{\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\mathbbm{R}\oplus V\,,

where
V
is finite-dimensional and contains only nilpotent elements.

It turns out that any Weil algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\cong\mathbbm{R}\oplus V
may be identified
[
MR91
, Thm. 3.17]
[
CR13
, Prop. 4.43]
with a quotient of some infinitesimal disk function algebra
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)
of Def.
2.4
– canonically up to an automorphism of
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

[
Kol08
, Lem. 1.7]
. Interpreted dually, this yields the following embeddings of general infinitesimal points into infinitesimal disks.

Lemma 2.8

(
Infinitesimal points as subspaces of infinitesimal disks).

Let
\mathbbm{D}
be an infinitesimally thickened point with Weil function algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\cong\mathbbm{R}\oplus V
of nilpotency order
l
, i.e.,
V^{l+1}=\{0\}
. There exists a subspace embedding into an infinitesimal disk

\iota\,:\,\mathbbm{D}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\,,

with the embedding map being the formal dual map of a quotient projection
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)/I\cong\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
, and
any other such embedding being equivalent up an automorphism of
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)

\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\iota}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\,.}

Proof.

Let
\{v^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
be a
minimal
generating set for the ideal of nilpotent elements
V\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
. It is not hard to see that, equivalently, this corresponds to a basis
\{v^{i}+V^{2}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
for the vector space
V/V^{2}
(cf.
[
Kol08
, Prop. 1.5]
). Define a surjective algebra morphism via the assignment on linear “generators”

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})

\displaystyle x^{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto v^{i}

extended to all of
C^{\infty}(M)
via Lem.
2.6
. By the
l
-order nilpotency of
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
, the kernel of this map contains

(x^{1},\cdots,x^{m})^{l+1}
, and so this projection factors through that the infinitesimal disk function algebra (
5
)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\pi\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})

\displaystyle\epsilon^{i}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}v^{i}\,.

In turn, this yields the sought-after isomorphism of
\mathbbm{R}
-algebras

\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\;\cong\;\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)/\mathrm{ker}(\pi)\,.

Next, choosing any other minimal generating set
\{\,\widehat{v}^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
for
V
, or equivalently a basis
\{\,\widehat{v}^{i}+V^{2}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
for
V/V^{2}
corresponds to a linear map
\{v^{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{v}^{i}=a^{i}{}_{j}v^{j}\}_{i,j=1,\cdots,m}
. This yields a different isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\widehat{\pi}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\;\widehat{\cong}\;\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)/\mathrm{ker}(\widehat{\pi})

\displaystyle\epsilon^{i}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\widehat{v}^{i}=a^{i}{}_{j}v^{j}\,,

but which is related to the former by defining the automorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\phi\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{i}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}(a^{-1})^{i}{}_{j}\epsilon^{j}

making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\,.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)/\mathrm{ker}(\pi)\,\cong\,\,\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\,\widehat{\cong}\,\,\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)/\mathrm{ker}({\widehat{\pi}})}

Interpreting the above algebra morphisms in the formal dual sense completes the proof.
∎

The dimension
m
of the vector space
V/V^{2}
, hence the minimal number of nilpotent generators of any Weil algebra, is known as the
width
of the algebra. From our dual perspective, given that this corresponds to the dimension of the corresponding ambient infinitesimal disk
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
, we should think of this invariant as the (infinitesimal) dimension of the corresponding thickened point
\mathbbm{D}
. Similarly, any generator
v^{i}
for
V
contained in a minimal such set should be thought of as a kind of “linear” coordinate function on
\mathbbm{D}
. Thus, it is instructive to think of the embeddings of Lem.
2.8
being related by an “infinitesimal” general linear transformation inside the corresponding disks, descending from the ambient finite linear transformation
(a^{i}{}_{j}):\mathbbm{R}^{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{m}
.

Remark 2.9

(
Infinitesimal disks vs. points).

For simplicity of presentation, we will often not explicitly consider these extra infinitesimal points in most of this text, and focus instead only on infinitesimal disks. All results and proofs developed herein may be straightforwardly generalized to this general case of infinitesimal points by recalling that any such embeds into an infinitesimal disk via Lem.
2.8
.

2.2
Sheaves over thickened probes

We are now sufficiently equipped to enlarge our original site of probe spaces
\mathrm{CartSp}

[
Part I
]
within the (opposite) category of commutative algebras, so as to include these infinitesimal points and their products with Cartesian spaces (hence

thickened
versions of Cartesian spaces).

Definition 2.10

(
Thickened Cartesian spaces).

The category of
(infinitesimally) thickened Cartesian spaces

\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}

is defined as the opposite category to the full subcategory of CAlg
R
consisting of objects of the form

\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;:=\;C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\,,

for
\mathbbm{D}
any infinitesimally thickened point.

In summary, we denote objects in ThCartSp by
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
and consider maps between them as formally dual to ‘pullbacks’ between their function algebras in CAlg
R
. Intuitively,
one thinks of
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
as the usual Cartesian space, supplied with an extra ‘halo of infinitesimal directions’ around each point
x\in\mathbbm{R}^{k}
. This category becomes a site by defining the (Grothendieck) coverage to be that of (differentiably) good open covers
\big\{U_{i}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\iota_{i}}}\mathbbm{R}^{k}\big\}_{i\in I}
, extended trivially along infinitesimal directions, and hence of the form

\displaystyle\Big\{U_{i}\times\mathbbm{D}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\;\iota_{i}\times\mathrm{id}\;}}\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\Big\}_{i\in I}\,.

(6)

There is an obvious relation between the two site categories
\mathrm{CartSp}
and
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
. That is, the embedding of sites

\displaystyle\iota:\mathrm{CartSp}

\displaystyle\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{ThCartSp}

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{R}^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{0}(0)\cong\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\{*\}\cong\mathbbm{R}^{k}\

and the projection that forgets the infinitesimal halo

\displaystyle p:\mathrm{ThCartSp}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{CartSp}

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathbbm{R}^{k}\,,

in fact, form an
adjunction

\iota\quad\dashv\quad p\,.

Namely
there is a canonical bijection

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}\big(\iota(\mathbbm{R}^{k}),\mathbbm{R}^{l}\times\mathbbm{D}\big)\;\;\cong\;\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CartSp}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k},\,p(\mathbbm{R}^{l}\times\mathbbm{D})\big)

natural in both
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
and
\mathbbm{R}^{l}\times\mathbbm{D}
. This is easily seen since
p(\mathbbm{R}^{l}\times\mathbbm{D})=\mathbbm{R}^{l}
, and due to the fact that
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
has no nilpotent elements other than the zero-function
0
. Indeed, the latter implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}\big(\iota(\mathbbm{R}^{k}),\mathbbm{R}^{l}\times\mathbbm{D}\big)

\displaystyle\;\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{l})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}),\,C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\big)\,

\displaystyle\;\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{l}),\,C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\big),

since any nilpotent element of
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{l}\otimes\mathbbm{D})\cong C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{l})\oplus\big(C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{l})\otimes W\big)
is necessarily mapped to
0\in C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
, and hence the adjunction bijection result follows. See for instance
[
KS17
]
for more implications of this adjunction.

Having described our new notion of thickened and smooth probe-spaces, and following the lines of logic of defining generalized spaces by the plots carved out by probing them with simpler spaces (see
[
Part I
, §2.1]
[
Sc24
]
[
Gi25
]
),
we immediately arrive at our definition of infinitesimally thickened smooth sets.

Definition 2.11

(
Thickened smooth sets).

The category of
(infinitesimally) thickened smooth sets
, or
thickened generalized smooth spaces
, is defined to be the (gros) sheaf topos

\displaystyle\mathrm{ThSmthSet}\;:=\;\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{ThCartSp})\,,

(9)

i.e., sheaves on thickened Cartesian spaces with respect to (differentiably) good open covers (
6
).

As with the case of smooth sets, thickened smooth sets are defined entirely in an
operational
manner, by consistently answering the question:

‘What are the ways we can probe the would-be space smoothly with Cartesian spaces,

(infinitesimally) thickened points and more generally thickened Cartesian spaces?’

To stress the point further: Sheaves over plain Cartesian spaces
define
the notion of smoothness of the would-be smooth generalized spaces, by giving meaning to what it means to smoothly probe them. Similarly, sheaves over thickened Cartesian spaces

define
the notion of smoothness
and
their infinitesimal nature, by giving meaning to what it means to (smoothly) probe them with simple test spaces of finite and infinitesimal extent.

Remark 2.12

(
Petit sheaf characterization).

The categorical sheaf condition under the coverage (
6
) is related to the usual topological sheaf condition in the following sense: A presheaf
\mathcal{F}:\mathrm{ThCartSp}^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
is a thickened smooth set if and only if
\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
defines a sheaf on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
, in the topological sense, for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{CartSp}
and every infinitesimal point
\mathbbm{D}
.

Of course, the Yoneda embedding (see e.g.
[
Part I
, Prop. 2.4]
) now applies for our thickened site and sheaves over it, yielding a fully faithful embedding
4
4

4

For a generic sheaf category
\mathrm{Sh}(C)
with respect to a chosen coverage on
C
, it is plausible that under the Yoneda embedding not every object of the site
C
satisfies the sheaf (‘gluing’) condition. In other words, it is not generally guaranteed that the Yoneda embedding factors through the corresponding full sheaf subcategory
y:C\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(C)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PreSh}(C)
. Site categories with the property of representables being sheaves, and not just presheaves, are called
“subcanonical”
. Both
\mathrm{CartSp}
and
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
with their corresponding good open coverages form examples of subcanonical sites.

\displaystyle y\;:\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}

(10)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}(-,\,\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}),\,\mathcal{O}(-)\big)|_{\mathrm{ThCartSp}}\,,

which allows one to view thickened Cartesian spaces as special instances of thickened smooth sets.

Examples of thickened smooth sets.

Following Def.
2.10
, one may define an analogous category of (infinitesimally) thickened smooth manifolds
5
5

5

There is a more general definition of thickened manifolds which are only (petit)
locally
isomorphic to the product form
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
(cf.
[
Ko06
]
, therein termed “formal manifolds”). This is in complete analogy to the case of super-manifolds being only locally products of super-Cartesian spaces
\mathbbm{R}^{k,q}=\mathbbm{R}^{k,0}\times\mathbbm{R}^{0,q}
. It can be shown in a similar manner that sheaves over these “formal manifolds” also yield an equivalent topos.

\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}\,,

with objects denoted by
M\times\mathbbm{D}
being formal duals of the corresponding algebras
C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
. Hence, we could as well consider thickened generalized smooth spaces as the sheaf category
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd})
over thickened smooth manifolds with the coverage of being that of (differentiably) good open covers (
6
). However, this category is precisely equivalent to that of thickened smooth sets (Def.
2.11
), in a manner completely analogous to how sheaves over plain smooth manifolds are equivalent to smooth sets (see discussion around
[
Part I
, Def. 2.1]
). In more detail, the pullback along the inclusion
\iota:\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}
defines an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle\iota^{*}\,:\,\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp})\,.

(11)

This is essentially because any (thickened) manifold
M\times\mathbbm{D}
is always covered by a (differentiably good) open cover

\{U_{i}\times\mathbbm{D}\hookrightarrow M\times\mathbbm{D}\}_{i\in I}
of (thickened) Cartesian spaces. In topos theory language,
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
is a
dense sub-site
of
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}
.

Example 2.13

(
Thickened manifolds as thickened smooth sets).

It follows that the composition which views a thickened smooth manifold as a thickened smooth set
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}\xrightarrow{y}\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd})\xrightarrow{i^{*}}\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp})
, abbreviated by

\displaystyle y\;:\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}

(12)

\displaystyle M\times\mathbbm{D}

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}}(-,\,M\times\mathbbm{D})|_{\mathrm{ThCartSp}}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}),\,\mathcal{O}(-)\big)|_{\mathrm{ThCartSp}}\,,

is also fully faithful.

The same formula can be used to view smooth (second coundable and Hausdorff) manifolds
with boundary and corners
as thickened smooth sets (see Appendix
4.2
for details).

Example 2.14

(
Manifolds with corners).

For any
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}
, locally modelled on
\mathbbm{R}^{k,l}:=\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times[0,\infty)^{\times l}
, there is a corresponding thickened smooth set defined by

y(M)\,:=\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(C^{\infty}(M),\,\mathcal{O}(-)\big)|_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}

where
C^{\infty}(M)
is algebra of
smooth
functions
6
6

6

Recall, a function
f:M\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
on a manifold with boundary / corners is smooth if it locally smooth with respect to any chart. Namely, if at any point
x\in M
and any chart
\psi:\mathbbm{R}^{k,l}\xrightarrow{\sim}U_{x}\hookrightarrow M
around it, the corresponding composition
f\circ\psi:\mathbbm{R}^{k,l}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
extends to a smooth function
\widetilde{f}:\mathbbm{R}^{k+l}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
between Cartesian spaces, in the usual sense analytical sense;
see
[
Jo19
]
[
FSJ24
]
[
Me96
]
for more details on these concepts.

on
M
. The proof that this presheaf satisfies the sheaf condition with respect to (
6
) can be found in Thm.
4.14
, where we also show that the functor

y\,:\,\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}

is, in fact,
fully faithful
. Hence, manifolds with corners are represented “from outside”, since these also embed fully faithfully into commutative algebras (Prop.
4.13
).

Moreover, the same formula yields a thickened smooth set corresponding to any
closed
subset
|K|\subset\mathbbm{R}^{n}
, viewed as a formal smooth space
K
dual to the subalgebra of functions on
|K|
which extend to smooth functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{n}
(cf. (
104
)).

Example 2.15

(
Smooth closed subspaces).

For any formal “smooth space”
K\in\mathrm{CAlg}^{\mathrm{op}}
dual to the algebra of functions

C^{\infty}(K)\,\cong\,C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{n})/I_{K}\,,

where
|K|\subset\mathbbm{R}^{n}
is a closed subset and
I_{K}
is the ideal of smooth functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{n}
which vanish along
|K|
, there is a corresponding thickened smooth set defined by
7
7

7

Strictly speaking, for the following to coincide with the corresponding notion using
C^{\infty}
-algebra morphisms,
K
should be dense in the sense that the closure of its interior is K itself,
\mathrm{Cl}(\mathrm{Int}(X))=X
. Otherwise, one may simply define
C^{\infty}(K):=C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{n})/I_{K}^{\infty}
where
I_{K}^{\infty}
is the ideal of smooth functions with all derivatives vanishing along
K
(cf. Lem.
4.15
).

y(K)\,:=\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(C^{\infty}(K),\,\mathcal{O}(-)\big)|_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}\,.

The sheaf condition for the presheaf
y(K)
holds precisely as in Thm.
4.14
.

Remark 2.16

(
On Yoneda notation).

To ease the heavy notation, we will often omit the Yoneda embedding symbols from Eqs. (
10
) (
12
) and Examples
2.14
,
2.15
, as it will be clear from the context in which category we consider these objects. We will explicitly use the Yoneda symbols only when they are necessary and/or instructive.

Another immediate example of a thickened smooth set is the “moduli space of de Rham
n
-forms”
\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}
, which we may naturally extend from its purely smooth set incarnation (
[
Part I
, Def. 2.29]
) to a working definition in the current context.
In algebro-geometric terms, the infinitesimal
\mathbbm{D}
-plots of
\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}
are given by the ‘‘Kähler differential forms’’
8
8

8

It is not hard to check explicitly that this is the same as Kähler forms on
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
as a
C^{\infty}
-algebra (Rem.
2.18
, ftn. 3, Def.
4.4
), as it is finitely generated (cf.
[
DK84
, §2]
[
Jo19
, §5]
). This latter notion is formally the correct one in our context, as it also recovers the traditional differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
(while the
\mathbbm{R}
-algebraic one does not). See Appendix
4.4
for more on these details.
on the finitely generated
\mathbbm{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
. Explicitly,

\displaystyle\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\;:

\displaystyle=\;\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\cdot(\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{m})\big/\mathrm{d}(\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{m})^{l+1}\,,

with the extra variables
\{\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
being of degree 1, where the equivalence relation is the induced one
0=\mathrm{d}(\epsilon^{i_{1}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l+1}})=\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{i_{1}}\cdot\epsilon^{i_{2}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l+1}}+\cdots+\epsilon^{i_{1}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{i_{l+1}}
. More generally, this means its
\mathbbm{D}
-plots
are given by the
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
-module freely generated in the formal variables
\{\mathrm{d}v\}_{v\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})}
subject to the induced algebra relations via
\mathrm{d}(v_{1}\cdot v_{2})=\mathrm{d}v_{1}\cdot v_{2}+v_{1}\cdot\mathrm{d}v_{2}
,
\mathrm{d}(v_{1}+v_{2})=\mathrm{d}v_{1}+\mathrm{d}v_{2}
,
\mathrm{d}r=0
for
r\in\mathbbm{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
. That is,

\displaystyle\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbbm{D})\,:=\,\mathrm{Span}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})}\big\{\mathrm{d}v\,|\,v\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\big\}\big/\mathrm{d}\big(p(v^{1},\cdots,v^{n})\big)=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial p}{\partial x^{i}}(v^{1},\cdots,v^{n})\cdot\mathrm{d}v^{i},

(13)

where
p\in\mathrm{Pol}(\mathbbm{R}^{n})
is any polynomial in
n
-variables. As expected, this reduces to the above simplified form for
\mathbbm{D}=\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
. Along the lines of Lem.
2.8
, this can take a more explicit form for a general infinitesimal point
\mathbbm{D}
, if necessary, via any identification
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\cong\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)/I
whereby

\displaystyle\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}\big(\mathbbm{D}\big)\;\cong\;\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}\big)\cdot(\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{m})\,/\,\mathrm{d}I\,.

(14)

Combining appropriately with the smooth
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of
\mathbold{\Omega^{1}}
yields the definition the thickened moduli space of de Rham forms.

Example 2.17

(
Thickened moduli space of de Rham forms).

The
thickened moduli space of de Rham
1
-forms

\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}
is the thickened smooth sets with
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots

\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;:=\;\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\;\oplus\;C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}(\mathbbm{D})\,,

Similarly, the
moduli space of
n
-forms

\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}
is the thickened smooth set with
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots

\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;:=\;\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathcal{O}\left(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\right)}\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\big)\,.

By construction, as with the smooth set case
[
Part I
, Lem. 2.31]
these thickened smooth sets yield a DGCA structure internal to
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}

\big(\mathbold{\Omega}^{\bullet},\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}},\,\wedge\big)\;\;\in\;\;\mathrm{DGCA}_{\mathbbm{R}}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet})\,.

which can be, yet again, used to define a notion of
de Rham forms
on any
thickened
smooth set
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
via (cf.
[
Part I
, Def. 2.32]
)

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})\,:=\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F},\,\mathbold{\Omega}^{\bullet})\,.

(15)

Remark 2.18

(
Variants of thickened sites).

(i)

There are several different variants of sites of smooth and infinitesimally thickened probe-spaces one may consider,
9
9

9

Namely these choices correspond to full subcategories of the category of “
smooth loci
”, the formal dual category of the full subcategory of finitely generated
C^{\infty}
-algebras (Def.
4.4
), i.e., of the form
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})/I
for some ideal
I\subset\mathbbm{R}^{k}
and
k\in\mathbbm{N}
. The choices are determined by conditions imposed on the allowed ideals
I
, but also by different choices of coverages one may supply the probe-category with
[
MR91
]
.
and hence many different categories of sheaves that serve as “well-adapted models” for synthetic differential geometry.

(ii)

This means that the category of smooth manifolds
\mathrm{SmthMfd}
embeds fully faithfully in any such well-adapted sheaf topos
\iota:\mathrm{SmthMfd}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
, while preserving transversal intersections/pullbacks among a couple further technical axioms
(see e.g.
[
Ko06
]
). The existence of different models for synthetic differential geometry guarantees that several abstract arguments and universal constructions may be equally interpreted in any of these categories of generalized spaces. Thus, these categories share several properties, but may in fact differ in more subtle technical details and not be actually equivalent. A detailed analysis of several such sites, their categories of sheaves, and their interrelations has been carried out in
[
MR91
]
.

(iii)

In this manuscript, we focus on arguably the simplest of these sites, with the least amount of probe spaces, and the corresponding sheaf topos
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
being equivalent to the “Cahiers topos” (Thm.
4.7
, Cor.
4.8
) of Dubuc
[
Du79
, §4]
[
KR87
]
, since this seems to be sufficiently rich for most purposes of (local) Lagrangian field theory. An instance where a more general choice of thickened site might be more appropriate, from a purely mathematical point of view, is to make manifest the full “classifying” property of
\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}
as defined above,
10
10

10

Alternatively, there exists a different, and more abstract, extension of the classifying space from smooth sets. Namely, one may assign instead to any probe
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
the set of “fiber-wise linear maps” of thickened smooth sets,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}^{\mathrm{fib.lin.}}\big(T(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}),\,y(\mathbbm{R})\big)
, from its synthetic tangent bundle (Def.
2.24
, Ex.
2.26
) into the real numbers. The resulting thickened smooth set satisfies the full classifying property over
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
. See Appendix
4.4
for more on this.
along the lines original sources of
[
La80
, §3]
[
DK84
, §8]
(described therein in more abstract terms, hence perhaps somewhat underappreciated). We review the technicalities of this matter in a brief manner in the Appendix
(
section

4.4
), as it is not directly relevant to the purposes of local Lagrangian field theory; This being due to the fact that local forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
may still be identified with certain maps into
\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}
from Def.
2.17
(see Rem.
3.15
).

2.3
Thickened mapping spaces and the synthetic tangent bundle

Since our thickened smooth sets form a (pre-)sheaf category, there exists a natural mapping space construction for any pair of thickened smooth sets. This is witnessed by the internal hom functor

\big[-,-\big]\;:\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}^{op}\times\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\;\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,,

which is defined exactly by the same formula as in that of smooth sets
[
Part I
]
, and in fact as for any (pre)sheaf category
[
MLM94
]
. We recall its defining formula for completeness, now in thickened smooth sets.

Definition 2.19

(
Thickened smooth mapping space).

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
, the
(thickened) smooth mapping space

[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}]\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
is defined by

\big[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}\big](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;:=\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\times\mathcal{G},\,\mathcal{F}\big)\,.

Crucially, thickened mapping spaces satisfy the corresponding internal hom property (or Exponential Law) property,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathcal{X},\,[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}]\big)\;\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{G},\,\mathcal{H}\big)\,,

(16)

naturally in
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
. This too, is proved using the fact that any thickened smooth set may be written as a colimit of thickened Cartesian spaces (representables), verbatim as in the case of smooth sets (
[
Part I
, p. 12]
), and in fact as in any (pre)sheaf category (
[
MLM94
, p. 42]
). The internal hom construction allows us to consider infinitesimally thickened mapping spaces between any two manifolds.

Example 2.20

(
Thickened mapping space of manifolds).

Let
M,N\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
. The
thickened (smooth) space of maps
from
M
to
N
given by

\displaystyle[M,\,N]\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\big)

\displaystyle\;:=\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big((\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\times M,\,N\big)

\displaystyle\;\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(C^{\infty}(N),\,C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\big)\,,

where
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\in\mathrm{ThCartSp}
is any infinitesimally thickened Cartesian probe-space.

In field theoretic terms, this neatly encodes the smooth
and
infinitesimal nature of the (off-shell) space of fields for a “
\sigma
-model” with domain
M
and target
N
. Similarly, more general field spaces consisting of sections of an arbitrary field fiber bundle
F\rightarrow M
, viewed originally simply as smooth sets in

[
Part I
, Def. 2.12]
, are promoted to thickened smooth sets by reinterpreting their defining diagram within
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
.

Definition 2.21

(
Thickened smooth set of sections).

Let
\pi:F\rightarrow M
be a fiber bundle of smooth manifolds. The thickened smooth set of sections
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\in\mathrm{SmoothSet}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;:=\;\big\{\phi^{k,\epsilon}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\rightarrow F\;|\;\pi\circ\phi^{k,\epsilon}=p_{M}\big\}\,,

(17)

where
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\in\mathrm{ThCartSp}
and
p_{M}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\rightarrow M
is the projection onto
M
. Explicitly, these consist of (formal duals of) algebra maps
(\phi^{k,\epsilon})^{*}:C^{\infty}(F)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
such that
the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{C^{\infty}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi^{k,\epsilon})^{*}}
\textstyle{C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})}
\textstyle{C^{\infty}(M)\;.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}}

The thickened bosonic field space above may be equivalently identified as a pullback / fiber product construction (cf. discussion above
Rem 2.13 of
[
Part I
]
), computed in thickened smooth sets

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[M,F]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{M}\cong*\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[M,M]\,,}

since we have already extended the three corners of the diagram to thickened smooth sets. In more detail, the mapping spaces on the right column are via Ex.
2.20
, while the bottom embedding is defined probe-wise by sending the (unique)
(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
-plot of the point
\mathrm{id}_{M}\cong*
to the projection-plot
p_{M}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\rightarrow M
inside

[M,\,M](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
, for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
.

As with any (pre)sheaf category, this is computed as the probe-wise fiber product in the category of sets, for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
, and immediately recovers the plots of Def.
2.21
. The top arrow then exhibits the field space of sections as a (thickened smooth) subspace of the mapping space

\mathcal{F}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}[M,F]\,,

as expected by the point-set inclusion
\Gamma_{M}(F)\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}(M,F)
. We note that, as with
[
Part I
, Rem. 2.14]
, field spaces of sections should really be viewed as also being (petit) sheaves over
\mathrm{Open}(M)
, hence forming overall a sheaf

\displaystyle\mathbold{\Gamma}_{(-)}(F)\;:\;\mathrm{Open}(M)\times\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathrm{Set}\,

\displaystyle(U,\,\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathbold{\Gamma}_{U}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\,

with respect to the product coverage of sites, but we shall suppress this point throughout in favor of ease of exposition.

Synthetic tangent bundles.

The fruitful – and quite intuitive – interaction of infinitesimal spaces with the internal hom functor of thickened smooth sets is one of the main reasons for the thickening of our Cartesian site. For instance,
by Lem.
2.2
and the Yoneda embedding of Ex.
2.13
, the following
sets
are in canonical bijection

\displaystyle TM\,

\displaystyle\cong\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(C^{\infty}(M),\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)\!\Big)

\displaystyle\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,M\big)\,.

That is, maps of thickened smooth sets from the infinitesimal line
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
to a manifold
M
detect precisely its tangent vectors. By the same formula, however, one may define two thickened smooth set structures on the tangent bundle set
TM
, which could (a priori) be different: the first by embedding it as a manifold via Ex.
2.13
and
2.14
, denoted by
y(TM)
, and the second by the internal hom construction
\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,M\big]
given as in Def.
2.19
. The latter defines the
“synthetic tangent bundle”
of
M
, and in fact recovers the smooth structure of the traditional tangent bundle
TM
in the following sense.

Proposition 2.22

(
Synthentic tangent bundle of manifold).

Let
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}
be a manifold and
TM\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}
its traditional tangent bundle. Then
y(TM)\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
coincides with the synthetic tangent bundle of
M
,

y(TM)\,\cong\,\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,M\big]\;\;\in\;\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,.

Proof.

We only show the isomorphism at the level of smooth sets here, i.e., restricting the site to Cartesian spaces, as it is more straightforward to spell out. This also follows from our Prop.
4.20
which provides a slightly different proof, but we provide the following further simplified argument for expository purposes. The thickened probes require some more argumentation, which has been worked out for the boundaryless (
[
Du79
]
[
MR91
, Prop. 1.12]
) and boundary case in (
[
Re07
, Prop. 2.8]
) with the case of corners following with minimal modifications given our results from Prop.
4.13
and Thm.
4.14
. The
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of the synthetic tangent bundle are given by

\big[D^{1}(1),\,M\big]\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\big)=\big[D^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{R}^{k},\,M\big]\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(C^{\infty}(M),\,\mathcal{O}\big(D^{1}(1)\big)\otimes C^{\infty}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\big)\!\Big)\,,

An algebra morphism
C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}\otimes C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\cong_{\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbbm{R}}}C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\oplus\epsilon\cdot C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
is given, suggestively, by

(p,X_{p})\;:\;f\;\longmapsto\;p(f)+\epsilon\cdot X_{p}(f)\,.

Expanding the algebra morphism property, and using
\epsilon^{2}=0
,

(f_{1}\cdot f_{2})\;\longmapsto\;p(f_{1})\cdot p(f_{2})+\epsilon\big(p(f_{1})\cdot X_{p}(f_{2})+X_{p}(f_{1})\cdot p(f_{2})\big)\,.

The first component defines an algebra map
p:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
, and so dually a smooth map of manifolds
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\rightarrow M
by Prop.
2.1
. For each
x\in\mathbbm{R}^{k}
, we may compose with evaluation at x (also an algebra map) to land in
\mathbbm{R}\oplus\epsilon\cdot\mathbbm{R}
. Then the second component is identified as a tangent vector
X_{p(x)}
at
p(x)\in M
for each
x\in M
, as in Lem.
2.2
. In particular, these fit in a diagram as

\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X_{p}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{M}

of smooth manifolds. Equivalently, these are sections of the pullback bundle,
X\in\Gamma_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}(p^{*}TM)
, acting as derivations on the pullback algebra
p^{*}C^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
, or yet equivalently derivations
X_{p}:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
relative to the algebra morphism
p:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
, and hence are
smooth
sections – by the same reasoning of derivations
C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
being in bijection with
smooth
sections of
TM
(a fact which again relies on Hadamard’s Lemma
2.6
); for a proof see
[
Fl53
, §4]
.
On the other hand, a plot in
y(TM)
is given simply by

X\;\;\in\;\;y(TM)\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\big)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k},TM\big)\,,

i.e., a smooth map of manifolds
X:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\rightarrow TM
. By composing with the projection
\pi:TM\rightarrow M
, this is equivalent to a triangle as above. Hence the two sets of plots are in bijection, which is easily seen to be functorial under pullbacks of probe Cartesian spaces.
∎

Remark 2.23

(
Mapping out of infinitesimal points).

Working along the same lines it is not hard to see that maps out of infinitesimal disks of higher order and dimension detect higher order tangent bundles and variants thereof.

(i)

For instance, for
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}
, expanding explicitly the defining conditions gives

\displaystyle\big[\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1),\,M\big]\,\cong\,y(TM\times_{M}TM)\,\cong\,y(TM)\times_{M}y(TM)

(22)

as the fiber product of
TM
with itself. For instance, at the level of
*
-plots, this isomorphism is given by sending a pair of tangent vectors
(p,X^{1}_{p}):C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1}]/(\epsilon^{1})^{2}
and
(p,X^{2}_{p}):C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{2}]/(\epsilon^{2})^{2}
to (the formal dual) of

\displaystyle\big(p,X^{1}_{p},X^{2}_{p}\big)\;:\;C^{\infty}(M)

\displaystyle\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1},\epsilon^{2}]\big/(\epsilon^{1},\epsilon^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;p(f)+\epsilon^{1}\cdot X_{p}^{1}(f)+\epsilon^{2}\cdot X_{p}^{2}(f)\;.

Notice that this assignment defines an algebra map if and only if
\epsilon^{1}\cdot\epsilon^{2}=0
; namely it
cannot extend
a map from the product
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
into
M
(unless either of
X^{1,2}_{p}
vanishes).

(ii)

Similarly,
\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,M\big]\cong y\big(T(TM)\big)
is the second order tangent bundle, which may also be deduced abstractly by repeatedly applying the internal hom property (
16
) (along with Prop.
2.22
). Along the same lines,
\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(2),\,M\big]\cong y\big(J^{2}(\mathbbm{R},M)\big)
is the manifold of second order jets of curves in
M
and, analogously,
\big[\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l),\,M]\cong y\big(J^{l}(\mathbbm{R}^{k},M)\big)
is the manifold of
l
-jets of
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots in
M
.

More generally, for any infinitesimally thickened point
\mathbbm{D}
(Def.
2.7
) the mapping space into any manifold
M
is itself a manifold (Prop.
4.20
)
[
Ko81
, Thm. 3]
[
Re07
, Prop. 2.8]

\displaystyle\big[\mathbbm{D},\,M\big]\;\cong\;y(T_{\mathbbm{D}}M)\,,

(23)

where
11
11

11

The given sources show the result for the case of manifolds with boundaries. The cornered case for smooth plots is our Prop.
4.20
, with thickened case following as in
[
Ko81
, Thm. 3]
[
Re07
, Prop. 2.8]
with minimal modifications, given our Prop.
4.13
and Thm.
4.14
.

T_{\mathbbm{D}}M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}^{\mathrm{cor}}

is the “
Weil bundle
” over
M
with respect to the Weil algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
(see Appendix
section

4.2
), to be thought of as a kind of generalized tangent (or jet prolongation) bundle.

(iii)

Furthermore, as we shall see in
section

2.4
, mapping appropriately out of special infinitesimal (neighborhoods of) points canonically attached to points of manifolds
M
detects (or
defines
) smooth spaces of jets of sections of arbitrary fiber bundles over
M
.

The upshot in the identification of the tangent bundle
TM
with the mapping space
\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),M\big]
is that the latter construction may be applied for an arbitrary target
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
. Hence, following
[
La80
]
[
La98
]
, we may extend it to a definition of the “
synthetic tangent bundle
” of any thickened smooth set. The discussion and examples that follow will show that this is indeed a reasonable definition, making fully rigorous the intuition of tangent vectors being ‘first-order infinitesimal curves’, as often invoked in particular in the field theory literature (e.g.,
[
Part I
, Ex. 15]
).

Definition 2.24

(
Synthetic tangent bundle).

The
synthetic tangent bundle

T\mathcal{F}
of a thickened smooth set
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
is defined as the mapping thickened smooth set

T\mathcal{F}\,:=\,\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}\big]\;\;\in\;\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,.

The set of tangent vectors on
\mathcal{F}
is then given simply as the corresponding
*
-plots

\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}\big](*)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}\big)\,.

Crucially, this construction does not yield a bare set of tangent vectors but a fully-fledged (thickened) smooth set structure. The (unique) embedding map
\iota:*\cong\mathbbm{R}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
of the point into the infinitesimal line defines a natural projection

\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}:=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}\big]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\circ\iota}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\cong[*,\,\mathcal{F}]\,,}

given, under the Yoneda embedding, by precomposition with
\iota:*\rightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
. In the case of
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
being a smooth manifold, it is easy to see that under the isomorphism of Prop.
2.22
, this projection corresponds to the usual bundle projection
TM\rightarrow M
.

Furthermore, the construction of the synthetic tangent bundle forms a functor

T:=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,-\big]\;\;:\;\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,,

with smooth maps
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
mapped to

\displaystyle Tf\,:\,T\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}T\mathcal{G}

(28)

\displaystyle v^{k}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}f\circ v^{k}_{\epsilon}\,,

where
v^{k}_{\epsilon}
is a plot of tangent vectors, i.e.,
v^{k}_{\epsilon}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
making the composition above manifest. As such, it automatically satisfies the expected functoriality property

T(g\circ f)\equiv Tg\circ Tf\;\;:\;\;T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathcal{H}\,,

for any maps
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
and
g:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
. Hence
Tf:T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{G}
may be naturally interpreted as the differential (pushforward) of any map
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
