, and so we will often also denote it as
\mathrm{d}f:T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{G}
. Indeed, in the case of
\mathcal{F}=M,\,\mathcal{G}=N
being two finite-dimensional manifolds, this recovers precisely the traditional pushforward

\displaystyle Tf\equiv\mathrm{d}f\;:\;TM\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow TN\,,

(29)

as seen immediately (plot-wise) by the identification
12
12

12

For instance, at the point-set level and using the derivation point of view,
Tf
sends a derivation
(p,X_{p})\in T_{p}M\cong\mathrm{Der}_{p}\big(C^{\infty}(M)\big)
to the derivation
f\circ(p,X_{p})\in T_{f}(p)N\cong\mathrm{Der}_{f(p)}\big(C^{\infty}(N)\big)
acting as
h\mapstochar\rightarrow h\circ f\mapstochar\rightarrow(p,X_{p})(h\circ f)=\big(h\circ f(p),X_{p}(h\circ f)\big)
, which is precisely the definition of the traditional pushforward.
used in the proof of Prop.
2.22
.

Let us also note a crucial categorical property of the synthetic tangent functor that will be very useful in applications. Namely by its very definition (Def.
2.24
) and the internal hom property (
16
), it enjoys a left-adjoint functor

\displaystyle\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times-\big)\quad\dashv\quad T(-)\,.

(30)

In other words, there is a canonical bijection

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathcal{F},\,\mathcal{G}\big)\;\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F},\,T\mathcal{G})

natural in both
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 2.25

(
Infinitesimally linear spaces).

Note that any synthetic tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}=[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}]
enjoys a fiber-wise “
\mathbbm{R}
-module” structure via a canonical ‘scaling’ map

\displaystyle\cdot\;:\;\mathbbm{R}\times T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}

(31)

induced
13
13

13

In the “
internal language
” of the sheaf topos (see e.g.
[
Ko06
]
[
MR91
]
) this action is often denoted simply as
(r,v)\mapstochar\rightarrow r\cdot v
, where
r
and
v
are arbitrary (generalized) elements (i.e. plots) of
\mathbbm{R}
and
T\mathcal{F}
, and
r\cdot v(d):=v(r\cdot d)
for
d
any plot of
\mathbbm{D}
. More explicitly, this is defined as adjunct of the composition

\mathrm{ev}\circ(m\times\mathrm{id}_{T\mathcal{F}})\,:\,y\big(\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)\times T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow y(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1))\times T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

under (
30
), where in turn
\mathrm{ev}:y(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1))\times T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is the adjunct of the identity
\mathrm{id}_{T\mathcal{F}}:T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
under (
30
). Spelling out the above plot-wise yields the map

\big(r_{\epsilon}^{k},\,d_{\epsilon}^{k},\,\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}^{k}\big)\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}^{k}\circ\Big(\big(m(r_{\epsilon}^{k},d^{k}_{\epsilon})\times\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}}\big)\circ\Delta_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}}\Big)

where
\Delta_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\rightarrow(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\times(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
is the diagonal map, which justifies the internal language notation.

by the (Yoneda embedded) scaling map
m:\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\rightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
of the real line on the infinitesimal line.

(i)

This is given dually by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R})\otimes\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\longmapsto t\cdot\epsilon

where
t\in C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R})
is the coordinate (identity) function on
\mathbbm{R}
. Of course, the latter scaling map descends precisely from the traditional finite scaling map

m\,:\,\mathbbm{R}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}_{x}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}_{x}\,,

acting on points as
(t_{0},x_{0})\mapstochar\rightarrow t_{0}\cdot x_{0}
, and hence dually on the coordinate function (by pulling back) as
x\mapstochar\rightarrow t\cdot x
.

(ii)

In full generality, however, one is not able to supply a fiber-wise
\mathbbm{R}
-linear structure
+:T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
. Nevertheless, this is possible whenever the space
\mathcal{F}
satisfies a natural categorical property regarding the behavior of mapping spaces of infinitesimal disks
\mathbbm{D}^{n}(1)
into it, aptly called “infinitesimal linearity” (see e.g.
[
Ko06
, Prop. 7.2]
). In a bit more detail, recall the two canonical embedding maps

\iota_{1/2}\,:\,\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1)

given dually by the coordinate projections
\epsilon_{1}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon,\,\epsilon_{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow 0
and
\epsilon_{1}\mapstochar\rightarrow 0,\,\epsilon_{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon
, respectively. These yield the
cocone
diagram

\textstyle{{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1)\,,}

which, in turn, under the internal hom functor into
\mathcal{F}
yields the
cone
diagram

\textstyle{[\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1),\,\mathcal{F}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\circ\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{(-)\circ\iota_{2}}
\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\,.}

(iii)

Now, if the latter diagram is actually a
limit
cone, then the corresponding (unique) universal map into the fiber product of tangent bundles

k\,:\,[\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1),\,\mathcal{F}]\;\xlongrightarrow{\sim}T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}T\mathcal{F}

is an
isomorphism
(cf. Rem.
2.23
(i)
). This map may be interpreted as a canonical way to uniquely combine any pair of (plots of) tangent vectors
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
to (plots of) maps
\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
, and vice-versa.
14
14

14

Recall the explicit form of (
22
) as a further motivation for the appearance of
\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1)
, rather than
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
.
In such a situation, one defines a natural fiber-wise “addition” map of thickened smooth sets over
\mathcal{F}
via

+\;\;:\;\;T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}T\mathcal{F}\xlongrightarrow{\;\;k^{-1}\;\;}[\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1),\,\mathcal{F}]\xlongrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt(-)\circ\mathrm{Diag}\hskip 8.19447pt}[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}]=T\mathcal{F}\,,

where
\mathrm{Diag}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{2}(1)
is the “diagonal embedding” given dually by
\epsilon_{1}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon,\,\epsilon_{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon
.
The above addition map can then be easily shown to be
\mathbbm{R}
-linear with respect to the natural scaling action (
31
) (cf.
[
Ko06
, Prop. 7.2]
[
MR91
, Prop. 1.3]
). Its associativity, however, requires further that the cocone diagrams formed by all the higher-dimensional inclusions
\iota_{1/\cdots/n}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{n}(1)
, also becomes limit diagrams upon (internal) mapping into
\mathcal{F}
. The totality of the latter conditions is the definition of the “
infinitesimal linearity
” property of a thickened space
\mathcal{F}
.
15
15

15

Infinitesimal linearity is subsumed by a more general condition, called “
microlinearity
”
[
Lav96
, §2.3]
[
MR91
, §V.1]

(the notion is originally due to
[
Be80
]
), which states that a larger class of cocone diagrams should turn into limit diagrams upon internal mapping into such a “microlinear” space
\mathcal{F}
. This allows for categorically natural formulations of concepts from classical differential geometry, beyond the fiber-wise linearity of tangent bundles.

We will not need to employ the abstract details of these conditions in this text, since in the
field-theoretic spaces of interest this fiber-wise linear structure exists and is immediately apparent, as we shall see.
16
16

16

In particular, all smooth manifolds and infinitesimal points are
microlinear spaces. Moreover, the class of microlinear spaces is furthermore closed under limits and the formation of mapping spaces via the internal hom functor
[
MR91
, §V.1]
. These abstract results guarantee that the field-theoretic tangent bundles constructed in this text (Lem.
2.27
, Prop.
2.28
, Lem.
2.34
, Lem.
3.10
, Cor.
3.20
) will enjoy a fiber-wise linear structure.

The following is an exotic application of the synthetic tangent bundle construction (from the traditional differential geometric point of view). Namely, it provides a well-behaved (and intuitive) notion of a tangent bundle on infinitesimal disks and points, even if they have only one actual geometric point. Note, any attempt using finite smooth curves would instead, necessarily, yield a trivial tangent bundle.

Example 2.26

(
Tangent bundle of infinitesimal disks).

Viewing the thickened point
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
as a thickened smooth set, its synthetic tangent bundle is defined as

T\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\,:=\,\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big]\,,

i.e., the thickened smooth space with
(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
-plots

\displaystyle T\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\,:

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D},\,\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\,

\displaystyle\cong\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\,,\,\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)

\displaystyle\cong\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l))\,,\,\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1))\otimes C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\big)\,,

by the Yoneda embedding. By an application of Lem.
2.6
, it is not hard to explicitly check
17
17

17

This bijection is shown in
[
MR91
, Prop. 1.11]
where they consider only
C^{\infty}
-algebra maps. Explicitly, it is determined by sending the map which acts on generators as
\epsilon^{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow g^{i}(h,x,\epsilon)
to that acting as
\epsilon^{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow g(0,x,\epsilon)
and
y^{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow\partial_{h}g^{i}(0,x,\epsilon)
. By
Prop.
4.6
{\bf(ii)}
, these
C^{\infty}
-algebra hom-sets actually coincide with those of all
\mathbbm{R}
-algebra maps between the given algebras.
that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\big(\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l))\,,\,\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1))\otimes C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\big)\,\cong\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)\,,\,C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\Big)\,,

naturally in
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\otimes\mathbbm{D}
, where

\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)\,:=\,\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\otimes C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})\big/\big\{0=y^{i_{1}}\epsilon^{i_{2}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l+1}}+\epsilon^{i_{1}}y^{i_{2}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l+1}}+\dots+\epsilon^{i_{1}}\epsilon^{i_{2}}\cdots y^{i_{l+1}}\big\}\,,

with
\{y^{i}\}
being the linear coordinate functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{m}
, written suggestively as “functions on the tangent bundle
T\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
”. In terms of thickened smooth sets, the above equation says that

T\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\;\;\cong_{\,\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\;\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)\,,\,\mathcal{O}(-)\Big)\Big|_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}\,,

and so the tangent bundle to any infinitesimally thickened point
\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
is ‘‘represented from outside’’
18
18

18

T\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
is not an object in our site
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
, and so not representable in the strict sense.
by the (formal dual) space with function algebra

\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)
.

Intuitively, the
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)
factor of the algebra represents the function algebra on the base of
T\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\rightarrow\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)
, with its nilpotency representing the infinitesimal nature. On the other hand, the
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})
factor represents the functions on the fiber, signaling that even though the base is infinitesimal and has only one actual point, its tangent fiber is not infinitesimal, having infinitely many points. The quotient relation describes how the infinite extent fiber
\mathbbm{R}^{m}
is ‘attached’ to the infinitesimal base. Furthermore, the fiber possesses an obvious
\mathbbm{R}
-linear structure, witnessed dually as the map of function algebras

\displaystyle+^{*}\;:\;\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l))}\mathcal{O}\big(TD^{m}(l)\big)

(32)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{i}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\epsilon^{i}

\displaystyle y^{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto y_{1}^{i}+y_{2}^{i}\,,

where
\{y_{1}^{i}\}
and
\{y_{2}^{i}\}
correspond to the linear coordinates of each of the (different) copies of
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{m})
within
the codomain.

Since
T=[\mathbbm{D}^{1}\,,\,-]
has a left adjoint, it preserves limits and, in particular, direct products, and so

T\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\cong T(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\times T\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}.
Working as above, it follows that the synthetic tangent bundle of an arbitrary probe
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
is represented by the formal dual of

\mathcal{O}\Big(T\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\!\Big):=\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)\otimes C^{\infty}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{m}_{y}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\big)\!\Big/\!\big\{0=y^{i_{1}}\epsilon^{i_{2}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l+1}}+\epsilon^{i_{1}}y^{i_{2}}\cdots\epsilon^{i_{l+1}}+\dots+\epsilon^{i_{1}}\epsilon^{i_{2}}\cdots y^{i_{l+1}}\big\}\,,

with the two extra
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
-factors corresponding to the usual smooth functions on
T\mathbbm{R}^{k}\cong\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
. Furthermore, the formula from Eq. (
32
) straightforwardly generalizes to a fiber-wise
\mathbbm{R}
-linear structure on
T\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}(l)\big)
. Moreover, one can work similarly, under any identification of Lem.
2.8
, to show that the tangent bundle
T(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
of thickened Cartesian space has a fiber-wise linear structure, described dually by a further quotient of the above algebras. In fact, it is further true that all objects in our site are actually microlinear (Rem.
2.25
)
[
Ko06
, Prop 17.6]
.

We now show how the synthetic tangent bundle construction immediately recovers the correct notion of tangent bundles on the infinite-dimensional field spaces arising in physics. We first show this for the
\sigma
-model field spaces
[M,N]
from Ex.
2.20
.

Lemma 2.27

(
Manifold mapping space tangent bundle).

For any smooth manifolds
M,N\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
, the synthetic tangent bundle of the thickened smooth mapping space
[M,\,N]\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
is naturally isomorphic to
[M,\,TN]
,

T\big[M,\,N\big]:=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),[M,N]\big]\;\;\cong\;\;\big[M,\,TN\big]\,.

Proof.

We provide two proofs, the first being abstract and resting on universal properties and Prop.
2.22
, with the second being more explicit by working plot-wise. Firstly, recall the internal hom property (
16
)

\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,[M,N]\big]\cong\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times M,\,N\big]\,,

by which the identification follows via the sequence of (natural) isomorphisms

\displaystyle T\big[M,N\big]:

\displaystyle=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,[M,N]\big]\cong\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times M,\,N\big]

\displaystyle\cong\big[M\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,N\big]\cong\big[M,\,[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,N]\big]

\displaystyle\cong\big[M,TN\big]\,,

where we used the internal hom property (
16
), the symmetric monoidal structure of
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}
(or
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
) via the direct product, followed again by the internal hom property, and finally the canonical identification of Prop.
2.22
.

Alternatively, being more explicit, by the internal hom property the
*
-plots of
T[M,N]
are given by

\displaystyle T[M,N](*)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times M,\,N\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(C^{\infty}(N),\,C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)\!\Big)\,.

Now, precisely as in the proof of Prop.
2.22
, an algebra map

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(N)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto f\circ\phi+\epsilon\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}(f)\,,

i.e., determined by a pair of maps
(\phi,\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}):M\rightarrow N\times TN

such that the diagram

\textstyle{TN\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{N}

commutes. Equivalently, this is simply a section of the pullback bundle
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in\Gamma_{M}(\phi^{*}TN)
, recovering with standard notion of tangent vectors on mapping spaces of manifolds while avoiding analytical technicalities. Similarly, the
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots are given by

\displaystyle T\big[M,\,N\big](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})

\displaystyle\cong\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times M,\,N\big](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big((\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times M,\,N\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\Big(C^{\infty}(N),\,C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\otimes\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)\!\Big)\,.

Computing analogously, algebra maps
C^{\infty}(N)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\otimes\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)
can be checked to correspond (dually) to pairs of
(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
-parametrized maps
(\phi^{k,\epsilon},\,\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}):\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow N\times TN
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{TN\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}
\textstyle{N\mathrlap{\,.}}

Such diagrams are completely determined by the diagonal maps
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\rightarrow TN
, with the bottom maps always recovered as the compositions
\phi^{k,\epsilon}=\pi\circ\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}
. But these diagonal maps are precisely the defining plots of
[M,TN]

\displaystyle\big[M,TN\big](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}):

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M,\,TN\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthMfd}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M,\,TN\big)\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

In this case, the fiber-wise
\mathbbm{R}
-linear structure of
T[M,N]\cong[M,TN]
becomes immediately apparent as that inherited by the target
TN
. The synthetic tangent bundle construction also immediately recovers the tangent bundle to field spaces of sections (
[
Part I
, Def. 2.19]
), generalizing the above result.

Proposition 2.28

(
Synthetic tangent bundle of field space).

The synthetic tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}:=[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}]
of a thickened smooth field space
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)
,

T\mathcal{F}\,\cong\,\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)\;\;\in\;\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,,

where
VF\rightarrow F\xrightarrow{\pi}M
is the vertical fiber bundle corresponding to the field bundle
\pi:F\rightarrow M
.

Proof.

This follows by universal properties. Namely, recall the pullback (limit) characterization of the field space from (
2.3
),

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[M,F]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{M}\cong*\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[M,M]\,.}

Since the tangent functor
T:=[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,-]
has a left adjoint (
30
), namely
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times(-)
, it preserves all (small) limits and in particular pullbacks. Thus, the synthetic tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}

is identified as the pullback

\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T[M,F]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\pi_{*})}
\textstyle{T(\mathrm{id}_{M})\cong T(*)\cong*\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T[M,M]\,.}

By Lem.
2.27
, the right vertical arrow is canonically identified with
[M,\,TF]\xlongrightarrow{(T\pi)_{*}}[M,TM]
, while the bottom arrow is canonically identified with the embedding of the point
*\hookrightarrow[M,TM]
using its zero-section incarnation. Explicitly, the latter is defined by sending the (unique)
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plot of
0_{M}\cong*
to the ‘zero-section’
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{M}}M\xrightarrow{0_{M}}TM
, which can be seen by applying the tangent functor adjunction plot-wise to the embedding
\mathrm{id}_{M}\cong*\hookrightarrow[M,M]
from diagram (
2.3
).

Thus, the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}
is equivalently the pullback

\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[M,\,TF]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(T\pi)_{*}}
\textstyle{0_{M}\cong*\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[M,\,TM]\,,}

where by computing object-wise this says that the
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots of
T\mathcal{F}
are given by

T\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})=\big\{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\in[M,\,TF](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\,\big|\,T\pi\circ\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}=0_{M}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{M}\big\}\,.

These are maps
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\rightarrow TF
making the diagram

\textstyle{TF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\;\mathrm{pr}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0_{M}}
\textstyle{TM\,}

commute, and hence in particular factoring through the vertical subbundle
VF\hookrightarrow TF
. By further composing on the right with the projection
TM\rightarrow M
, one sees that maps
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}_{\epsilon}}
comprise precisely of sections of the form

\textstyle{VF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{M}}
\textstyle{M}

over
M
, which are exactly the defining
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots of
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)
.
∎

Of course, composing a plot
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}
on the right with the projection
VF\rightarrow F
yields
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-parametrized sections of
F\rightarrow M
,

\phi^{k,\epsilon}:=\pi_{F}\circ\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\,.

That is, any plot of tangent vectors
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}
covers a (unique) plot of fields
\phi^{k,\epsilon}
, which realizes precisely the expected canonical projection of thickened smooth spaces from (
2.3
),

T\mathcal{F}\cong\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)\;\xlongrightarrow{\;\;(\pi_{F})_{*}\;\;}\;\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)=\mathcal{F}\,,

and hence justifying the notation for plots of the former. Furthermore, the content of
[
Part I
, Lem. 2.17, ftn. 12]
generalizes to the case of
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-parametrized plots, namely:

Lemma 2.29

(
Representing plots of tangent vectors via paths of plots).

For any plot of tangent vectors
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\in T\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
covering a plot of fields
\phi^{k,\epsilon}=\pi_{F}\circ\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})

\textstyle{VF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{F}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{M\,,}

there exists a parametrized section

over

, such that
\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{0}=\phi^{k,\epsilon}
and

. That is, the map

\displaystyle\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\partial_{t}\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}|_{t=0}

is surjective, for each probe
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}

Proof.

The case of (non-thickened)
\mathbbm{R}^{k}

, Lem. 2.17, ftn. 12]
. The case of an arbitrary thickened

In more detail, recall from Rem.
2.23
and Eq. (

) (see Prop.

is, in fact, canonically isomorphic to another fiber bundle
of manifolds
over

[\mathbbm{D},F]\,\cong\,T_{\mathbbm{D}}F\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow F\rightarrow M\,.

Similarly, applying the same mapping space construction to the (synthetic) tangent bundle

over

(Prop.

), and using the internal hom property we have

\displaystyle T_{\mathbbm{D}}(TF)\,

\displaystyle\cong\,\big[\mathbbm{D},[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),F]\big]\cong[\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),F]

\displaystyle\cong\,\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),[\mathbbm{D},F]\big]\,\cong\,T(T_{\mathbbm{D}}F)\,,

and since these are functorial with respect to the base projections maps onto

(and

), the corresponding vertical subbundle

must be preserved under the functor

(and hence as thickened smooth sets)

T_{\mathbbm{D}}(VF)\,\cong\,V(T_{\mathbbm{D}}F){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt}}T(T_{\mathbbm{D}}F).

Applying the internal hom property with respect to the
\mathbbm{D}
-factor of the
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-probe to of the diagram (
2.29
), this uniquely corresponds to an analogous diagram of
non-thickened

\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots

\textstyle{V(T_{\mathbbm{D}}F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{T_{\mathbbm{D}}F}}
\textstyle{T_{\mathbbm{D}}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\mathbbm{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{{\phi^{k,\mathbbm{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\mathbbm{D}}_{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}}
\textstyle{T_{\mathbbm{D}}M\,,}

where the bottom adjuct map necessarily maps

to an embedded submanifold of

, canonically diffeomorphic to the base

.
19
19

19

This can be seen analogously to the case of the tangent bundle

and the canonical embedding given by the zero section

.

Applying now the original result
[

, Lem. 2.17, ftn. 12]
yields a map
\phi^{k,\mathbbm{D}}_{t}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\rightarrow T_{\mathbbm{D}}F

. Finally, applying the

In other words, since
[\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t},\mathcal{F}](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D},\mathcal{F})\cong\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t})
, this exhibits the following sequence of projections (plot-wise surjections, hence epimorphisms)

\displaystyle\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F}):=\big[\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t},\,\mathcal{F}\big]

\displaystyle\;\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\qquad T\mathcal{F}\quad\;\;\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;\;\mathcal{F}

(39)

\displaystyle\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}

\displaystyle\;{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\;\partial_{t}\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}\big|_{t=0}\;\longmapsto\;\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t=0}

where
\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}\in\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
is an
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plot in the (thickened) path space of fields. Due to the first plot-wise surjection, one may represent arbitrary plots
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}
of the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}
by the derivative of a (non-unique) plot in paths of fields

\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\,=\,\partial_{t}\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}\big|_{t=0}\,,

just as with tangent vectors of finite-dimensional manifolds. Importantly, let us also note that although by the explicit plot-wise (and point-wise over M!) construction of the projection map
\partial_{t}|_{t=0}:\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
seems rather ad hoc from the categorical point of view of (purely) smooth sets (cf.
[
Part I
, Ex. 2.16]
), here it is easily seen that to coincide with the (internal) restriction of (plots of) paths along
\iota_{0}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}|_{t=0}\,\equiv\,\iota_{0}^{*}\;\;:\;\;\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:=\,[\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t},\,\mathcal{F}]\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}T\mathcal{F}\,:=\,\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{F}\big]\,,

(40)

just as with the case of finite-dimensional manifolds.

Indeed, to see this notice that restricting any path of plots
\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}\in\mathbold{P}\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\cong\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t})

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{M}}
\textstyle{M}

along
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\;\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}}\times\iota_{0}\;}}\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}

yields an
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
parametrized section of
F
over M. Equivalently by the internal hom property (

) with respect to the
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
, this is a section of

(Prop.

) which moreover, by induced the section condition, necessarily factors through

\textstyle{VF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{M}}
\textstyle{M\mathrlap{\,.}}

Computing the form of the dual map, as e.g., in the proof of Lem.

, then shows that it coincides precisely with the intuitive derivative of
\phi^{k,\epsilon}_{t}
at
t=0
.

Remark 2.30

(
General mapping space tangent bundles).

The diagrammatic categorical arguments in the proofs of Lem.
2.27
and Prop.
2.28
apply for arbitrary objects in
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
. That is, it follows identically that for any two thickened smooth spaces
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

T[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}]\;\cong\;[\mathcal{G},T\mathcal{H}]\,.

Similarly, for any ‘bundle’ map of thickened smooth spaces
\pi:\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, the synthetic tangent bundle to the space of sections
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{K})
is canonically identified as the pullback
T\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{K})\cong\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{G}}(V\mathcal{K})
, with the latter being defined as the pullback

\textstyle{\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{G}}(V\mathcal{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{G},\,T\mathcal{K}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(T\pi)_{*}}
\textstyle{0_{\mathcal{G}}\cong*\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{G},\,T\mathcal{G}]\,.}

The catch here is that in this generality, these constructions are quite formal in nature.
T\mathcal{H}
is defined simply as
\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\mathcal{H}\big]
which might not have a more ‘concrete’ description, and might even lack a fiber-wise linear structure.
Similarly
T\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{K})
might lack a fiber-wise linear structure, and hence the bottom map may not necessarily be an actual
zero
-section, but instead is defined simply as the (unique) adjunct
0_{M}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow T\mathcal{G}
of the projection
p_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
.

Having identified the appropriate notion of a tangent bundle on field spaces, we may now import the correct notion of a cotangent bundle from
[
Part I
, Def. 5.35]
, now defined directly within thickened smooth sets.

Definition 2.31

(
Variational cotangent bundle).

The
variational cotangent bundle

\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:T^{*}_{\mathrm{var}}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
of a thickened field space

\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
is defined as the (smooth, thickened) vector bundle

\textstyle{T^{*}_{\mathrm{var}}\mathcal{F}\,:=\,\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(\wedge^{d}T^{*}M\otimes V^{*}F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\,:=\,\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\,,}

where the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}
is given by postcomposition of (plots of) sections of
\wedge^{d}T^{*}M\otimes V^{*}F

with the (manifold) vector bundle projection
\pi_{F}:\wedge^{d}T^{*}M\otimes V^{*}F\rightarrow F
.

The crucial property of the tangent and variational cotangent bundle is the existence of a pairing on fibers over (plots of) field configurations

\displaystyle\langle-\,,\,-\rangle\quad:\qquad T\mathcal{F}\times

\displaystyle T^{*}_{\mathrm{var}}\mathcal{F}\;\;\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\Omega^{d}_{\mathrm{vert}}(M)\,:=\,\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(\wedge^{d}T^{*}M)

(41)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\,

\displaystyle,\,\mathcal{B}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\big)\;\longmapsto\quad\big\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\,,\,\mathcal{B}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}\big\rangle\,,

induced by composing pairs of plots tangent
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times M\rightarrow VF
and cotangent vectors
\mathcal{B}_{\phi^{k,\epsilon}}
over a common plot of fields
\phi^{k,\epsilon}\in\mathcal{F}
, point-wise over
M
, with the canonical fiber-wise duality pairing

VF\times\big(V^{*}F\otimes\wedge^{d}T^{*}M\big)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\wedge^{d}T^{*}M\,.

When further composed with integration
20
20

20

When well-defined, i.e. for
M
compact, or more generally for plots of fields with compact support along
M
.
(
99
), this yields a real-valued non-degenerate
21
21

21

In the sense of a pairing of
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
-modules, plot-wise for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\in\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
. See the proof of Thm.
3.17
for more details.
pairing of tangent vectors and (variational) cotangent vectors on field space within
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
, in direct analogy to the case of the tangent and cotangent bundle of a finite-dimensional smooth manifold. As naturally expected, this pairing appears precisely in the dynamical
variational
principle – which, as we shall see (Thm.
3.17
), still holds in
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
and hence serves as the core motivation for the above definition.

We include a further intuitively desired property of tangent vectors within thickened smooth sets. In
[
Part I
, Rem. 2.18]
, we pointed out that tangent vectors to field spaces do not
necessarily
define
\mathbbm{R}
-valued derivations of the full algebra of smooth functions
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
, if defined in the category of smooth sets as
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathcal{F},\,\mathbbm{R}\big)
. This peculiarity is automatically (and generally) cured in the category thickened smooth sets.

Lemma 2.32

(
Path derivations depend only on tangent vectors).

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a thickened smooth set and
\phi_{t}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathbbm{R},\,\mathcal{F})
a line plot in
\mathcal{F}
. Denote the smooth real-valued functions on
\mathcal{F}
by
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F},\,\mathbbm{R})
. Then the induced derivation

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathbbm{R}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(f\circ\phi_{t})\big|_{t=0}

depends only on the corresponding synthetic tangent vector
\phi_{\epsilon}:=\phi_{t}\circ\iota_{0}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Suppressing the mentioning of the Yoneda embedding symbol as before, we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{t}}
\scriptstyle{f\circ{\phi}_{t}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\,,}

where by precomposing with the canonical inclusion
\iota_{0}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}
we get

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{t}}
\scriptstyle{f\,\circ\,{\phi}_{t}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\,.}

The bottom composition
(f\circ\phi_{t})\circ\iota_{0}:\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
expands dually as

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R})

\displaystyle\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\big)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;f(\phi_{0})+\epsilon\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(f\circ\phi_{t})\big|_{t=0}\;,

while the upper composition
f\circ\phi_{\epsilon}=f\circ(\phi_{t}\circ\iota_{0})
manifestly depends only the corresponding tangent vector, and the result follows.
∎

This means that in the particular case of
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
being a thickened smooth field space, any tangent vector

\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}=\partial_{t}\phi_{t}|_{t=0}\quad\in\quad T\mathcal{F}(*)\cong\Gamma_{M}(VF)\,

defines a unique real-valued derivation, for any choice of representative line plot
\phi_{t}
(which exists by
[
Part I
, Lem. 2.17]
). This is in stark contrast with
[
Part I
, Rem. 2.18]
where the field space is viewed simply as a smooth set, ignoring its infinitesimal structure. Indeed, maps between
thickened
smooth sets ‘
respect the infinitesimal structure
’ and not only the smooth structure, thus guaranteeing the intuitive sought-after property that derivatives along smooth curves should depend only on the first order class of the curve. It follows similarly that vector fields on
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F})
on the field space
\mathcal{F}
, defined in the thickened smooth setting as sections (cf.
[
Part I
, Def. 2.20]
)

\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\,,}

define smooth derivations

\mathcal{Z}:C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})\,,

in contrast to the purely smooth setting of
[
Part I
, (33)]
.

Infinite jet bundles.

We close this section by importing the infinite jet bundle in the thickened smooth setting, and hence recovering its tangent bundle, too, via the synthetic tangent bundle construction. In
[
Part I
, §3.1]
, we have considered the infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F
as a limit in Frèchet manifolds and its smooth set incarnation
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FrMfd}}(-,J^{\infty}_{M}F)\in\mathrm{SmSet}
, which is equivalently the limit of the smooth set incarnations of the finite order jet bundles
J^{n}_{M}F\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
, i.e.,

\lim_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}y(J^{n}_{M}F)\;\;\in\;\;\mathrm{SmthSet}.

Following the above discussion, it is straightforward to promote the infinite jet bundle to a thickened smooth set, by considering instead each finite order jet bundle as a thickened smooth set (Ex.
12
), and computing the limit in thickened smooth sets.

Definition 2.33

(
Thickened infinite jet bundle).

The thickened infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F
of a fiber bundle
F\rightarrow M
of manifolds is given by

J^{\infty}_{M}F\,:=\,\lim_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}y(J^{n}_{M}F)\quad\in\;\;\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,.

By construction, this limit comes supplied with the (universal) projection maps

\displaystyle\pi^{\infty}_{n}\;:\;J^{\infty}_{M}F\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}J^{n}_{M}F

(42)

of thickened smooth sets. It is straightforward to see that the synthetic jet bundle

T\big(J^{\infty}_{M}F\big):=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,J^{\infty}_{M}F\big]

recovers that of
[
Part I
, Def. 4.1]
, however, computed in thickened smooth sets and hence also providing it with an infinitesimal structure.

Lemma 2.34

(
Infinite jet synthetic tangent bundle).

The synthetic tangent bundle
TJ^{\infty}_{M}F:=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,J^{\infty}_{M}F\big]
of an infinite jet bundle is canonically isomorphic to the limit of tangent bundles of finite jet bundles

TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\;\cong\;\;\lim_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}y(TJ^{n}_{M}F)\,.

Proof.

Once again, this follows since the tangent functor
T:=\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,-\big]
has a left adjoint and hence preserves (small) limits. More explicitly, we can see the result by standard properties of limits and (internal) hom functors. Suppressing the Yoneda embedding, the
(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})
-plots of the two thickened smooth sets are canonically (and functorially) identified as

\displaystyle\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),J^{\infty}_{M}F\big](\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;

\displaystyle=\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),J^{\infty}_{M}F\big)\;=\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\Big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),\,\lim_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}J^{n}_{M}F\Big)

\displaystyle\;\cong\;\lim_{Set}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\times\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),J^{n}_{M}F\big)\;\cong\;\lim_{Set}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\Big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D},\,\big[\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1),J^{n}_{M}F\big]\Big)

\displaystyle\;\cong\;\lim_{Set}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D},\,TJ^{n}_{M}F\big)\;\cong\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}\Big(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D},\,\lim_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}TJ^{n}_{M}F\Big)

\displaystyle\;\cong\;\lim_{\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}}TJ^{n}_{M}F(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})

where we used the fact that limits commute with the hom functor, the internal hom property and Prop.
2.22
.
∎

It follows similarly that the corresponding limit of the (pushforward) projection maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}_{n}\;:\;TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}TJ^{n}_{M}F

(43)

coincide with the synthetic pushforwards (
28
), i.e., acting via (internal) composition plot-wise
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\times(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F\xrightarrow{\pi^{\infty}_{n}}J^{n}_{M}F
.
We note that from this thickened point of view, the result of
[
Part I
, Lem. 4.2]
, which identifies tangent vectors on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
with first order classes of lines in
J^{\infty}_{M}F
, follows immediately as a corollary of Lem.
2.32
. We will see in
section

2.4
that there is a more natural way, categorically and intuitively speaking, to define the infinite jet bundle of
any
(thickened) smooth map
\pi:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
– which does indeed recover the above definition in the case of a finite-dimensional fiber bundle.

Representing plots of
J^{\infty}_{M}F
and its tangent bundle.

Note that the limit characterization of both
J^{\infty}_{M}F
and
T(J^{\infty}_{M}F)
means that their plots may be formally represented and manipulated precisely as in the purely smooth setting, essentially by carrying along the dependence on nilpotent elements corresponding to the infinitesimal thickening of the Cartesian probes. More explicitly, by definition,
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots of
J^{\infty}_{M}F
are compatible families of plots of
\{J^{n}_{M}F\}_{0\leq|I|}

J^{\infty}_{M}F(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D})\;\cong\;\Big\{\big\{s^{k,n}_{\epsilon}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\rightarrow J^{n}_{M}F\;\;\big|\;\;\pi^{n}_{n-1}\circ s^{k,n}_{\epsilon}=s^{k,n-1}_{\epsilon}\big\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\Big\}\,.

That is, any plot
s^{k}_{\epsilon}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
is dually completely determined by its (compatible) action on (a cover of) the local coordinate functions
\{x^{\mu},\,u^{a}_{I}\}_{0\leq|I|\leq n}
on each
J^{n}_{M}F
, hence by an infinite list of compatible assignments

\displaystyle x^{\mu}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}s^{\mu}(c^{k},\epsilon)

(44)

\displaystyle u^{a}_{I}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}s^{a}_{I}(c^{k},\epsilon)\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\,.

In this local representation, the limit projections
\pi^{\infty}_{n}:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow J^{n}_{M}F
from (
42
) act plot-wise by ‘forgetting’ the
|I|>n
members of the family, i.e., by forgetting assignment on coordinates
u^{a}_{I}
with
|I|>n
.

Analogously, by Lem.
2.34
,
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}
-plots of
TJ^{\infty}_{M}F
are compatible families of plots of
\{TJ^{n}_{M}F\}_{0\leq|I|}
, and hence any plot

\textstyle{TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}F}

covering a plot
s^{k}_{\epsilon}
of
J^{\infty}_{M}F
may be represented by an infinite formal sum

\displaystyle X_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}\;=\;X^{\mu}_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\Big|_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}\;+\;\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}Y^{a}_{I,s^{k}_{\epsilon}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{I}^{a}}\Big|_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}\,,

(45)

where
\big\{X^{\mu}_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}},Y^{a}_{I,s^{k}_{\epsilon}}\big\}_{0\leq|I|}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})
, which stands for the infinite list of compatible assignments on the corresponding coordinates on the tangent bundles
TJ^{n}_{M}F

\displaystyle x^{\mu}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}s^{\mu}(c^{k},\epsilon)

\displaystyle\dot{x}^{\mu}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}X^{\mu}_{s^{k}_{\epsilon}}(c^{k},\epsilon)

(46)

\displaystyle u^{a}_{I}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}s^{a}_{I}(c^{k},\epsilon)

\displaystyle\dot{u}^{a}_{I}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}}Y^{a}_{I,s^{k}_{\epsilon}}(c^{k},\epsilon)\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathbbm{D})\,.

In this local representation, the pushforward limit projections
\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}_{n}:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow J^{n}_{M}F
from (
43
) act plot-wise by ‘forgetting’ the
|I|>n
members of the family, i.e., by truncating the formal sum at
|I|=n
. The formal sum representation (
45
) also makes the fiber-wise (and plot-wise)
\mathbbm{R}
-linear structure of
TJ^{\infty}_{M}F
manifest, hence constituting a well-defined map of thickened smooth sets

+\;:\;TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\times_{J^{\infty}_{M}F}TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\,,

as intuitively expected.

2.4
Infinitesimal neighborhoods and jets of sections

We have seen (Lem.
2.2
) that tangent vectors on a finite-dimensional manifold M are detected by maps out of the
infinitesimal line
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)
. Another equivalent point of view of tangent vectors on
M\in\mathrm{Man}
, is as 1-jets of maps
\mathbbm{R}\rightarrow M
at
0\in\mathbbm{R}
,
or yet equivalently jets of sections of the trivial bundle
\mathbbm{R}\times M\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
at
0\in\mathbbm{R}
. By canonical
embedding of the infinitesimal line into the real line
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\iota_{0}}}\mathbbm{R}^{1}
(see (
4
)), Lem.
2.2
may be equivalently
be phrased as the bijection between jets of sections
J^{1}_{0\in\mathbbm{R}}(\mathbbm{R}\times M)
at
0\in\mathbbm{R}
of the base, with the set of maps

\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}\times M
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{1}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}}

commutes. The interpretation is in line with intuition: 1-jets of sections of
\mathbbm{R}\times M\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
at the origin are the same as sections over the first order
infinitesimal neighborhood of the origin. It follows similarly that
the analogous diagrams

\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{D}^{1}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}}

with
\mathbbm{D}^{1}(k)
instead will detect
k
-jets
J^{k}_{0\in\mathbbm{R}}(\mathbbm{R}\times M)
of curves in
M
, for any
k\in\mathbbm{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
That is,
k
-order jets of sections of
\mathbbm{R}\times M\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
at
0\in\mathbbm{R}
are precisely sections over out of the
k
-order
infinitesimal neighborhood at
0\in\mathbbm{R}
. We will show how this intuition naturally generalizes to the case of any
fiber bundle
F\rightarrow M
of manifolds, allowing to detect (or define) jets of sections as sections over infinitesimal neighborhoods
at a point in the base. In fact, a natural notion of an infinitesimal neighborhood in
any
thickened smooth sets allows one to define a notion of jets for maps between any two thickened smooth sets (Def.
2.42
). Moreover, the latter yields another way to recover the infinite jet bundle (Thm.
4.30
, due to
[
KS17
]
) with its (thickened) smooth structure from Def.
2.33
, directly in
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
, once again avoiding
any mention of infinite-dimensional manifold theory.

Remark 2.35

(
Manifolds with corners and infinitesimal neighborhoods).

The discussion that follows applies essentially verbatim for manifolds with boundaries and corners, due to our results from
section

4.2
(Prop.
4.13
, Thm
4.14
), with minimal or no modifications. For simplicity of presentation, we will not abstain from referring to cornered manifolds repeatedly.

Before stating the abstract definition of the infinitesimal neighborhood of a point (or plot) of an arbitrary thickened smooth set, we identify the
notion of an infinitesimal neighborhood at a point
p
in an arbitrary smooth manifold
M\in\mathrm{Man}
via the embedding
\mathrm{Man}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}^{op}_{\mathbbm{R}}
of Lem.
2.1
, extending the case of those of Cartesian spaces. For
p\in M
a point in a manifold,
we are after natural ‘infinitesimal spaces’ attached to
p\in M
that should serve as infinitesimal neighborhoods
in at
p\in M
.

As discussed in
section

2.1
, these ought to be the formal duals of
certain nilpotent algebras, now naturally associated to the point
p\in M
. Of course, there is a well-known collection of such algebras associated to the pair
(p,M)
.

Definition 2.36

(
Infinitesimal neighborhoods of manifolds).

Let
p\in M
be a point in a finite-dimensional manifold. For any
k\in\mathbbm{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,

(i)

the
k
-order
infinitesimal neighborhood at
p
, denoted by
\mathbbm{D}_{p,M}(k)
, is the formal dual of the jet algebra denoted by

\displaystyle J^{k}_{p}(M):=\Big\{j^{k}_{p}f=[f]\,\big|\,f\sim f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(M)\iff\partial_{|I|}f(p)=\partial_{|I|}f^{\prime}(p)\hskip 8.5359pt\forall k\geq I\geq 0\Big\}\quad\in\quad\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}\,,

based at
p\in M
, with the partial derivatives being computed in any local chart;

(ii)

the
\infty
-order neighborhood at
p
is given by the formal dual of the colimit of
k
-order neighborhoods in
\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}
, or dually as the limit of
k
-order infinitesimal neighborhoods in
\mathrm{CAlg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{op}
.
22
22

22

If such infinite limits of infinitesimal points were originally included in the site
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}
, since the Yoneda embedding preserves limits, it would be equivalently the limit in
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
. However, strictly speaking, being infinite-dimensional
J^{\infty}_{p}(M)
is not an example of a traditional Weil algebra (Def.
2.7
), hence only represented from the outside in
\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{SmthSet}
.

Tautologically, of course,
J^{k}_{p}(M)
coincides with the
k
-jets of sections of the trivial bundle
M\times\mathbbm{R}\rightarrow M
. The algebra structure is inherited by that of the fibers, that is
j^{k}_{p}(f)\cdot j^{k}_{p}(f^{\prime}):=j^{k}_{p}(f\cdot f^{\prime})
for any representatives
f,f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(M)
of the corresponding jet classes. For the case of the Cartesian space
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathbbm{D}^{d}(k)\cong\mathbbm{D}_{0,\mathbbm{R}^{d}}(k)
given dually by the (formal) Taylor expansion to order
k
, with respect to the canonical coordinates on
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle J^{k}_{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\;\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\;\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{d}(k)\big)=\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{d}]/(\epsilon^{1},\cdots,\epsilon^{d})^{k+1}

(47)

\displaystyle j^{k}_{0}f

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;\sum_{I=0}^{k}\frac{1}{I!}\partial_{|I|}f(0)\cdot\epsilon^{I}=f(0)+\partial_{i}f(0)\cdot\epsilon^{i}+\cdots\,,

by which we shall identify
\mathbbm{D}_{0,\mathbbm{R}^{d}}(k)
with
\mathbbm{D}^{d}(k)
from now on. Given a local chart
\phi:\mathbbm{R}^{d}\xrightarrow{\sim}U_{p}\hookrightarrow M
centered at
p\in M
, there is an induced isomorphism on jet algebras given by

\displaystyle j^{k}\phi^{*}\;:\;J^{k}_{p}(M)

\displaystyle\;\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\;J^{k}_{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong\mathcal{O}\big(\mathbbm{D}^{d}(k)\big)

(48)

\displaystyle j^{k}_{p}f

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;j^{k}_{0}(f\circ\phi)=f\circ\phi(0)+\partial_{i}(f\circ\phi)(0)\cdot\epsilon^{i}+\cdots\,,

which is, in fact, already implicit in Def.
2.36
of
J^{k}_{p}(M)
. In particular, any such isomorphism makes the nilpotent nature of
J^{k}_{p}(M)
apparent, giving an explicit splitting
J^{k}_{p}(M)\cong_{\mathrm{Vect}}\mathbbm{R}\oplus V
where
V
contains only nilpotent elements. Of course, this means that
j^{k}_{p}(f)\in J^{k}_{p}(M)
is nilpotent if and only if
f(p)=0
. Denoting the formal dual of this isomorphism by

j^{k}\phi\,:\,\mathbbm{D}^{d}(k)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\mathbbm{D}_{p,M}(k)\,,

the nilpotency of the latter algebra, together with the canonical ‘embedding’

\mathbbm{D}_{p,M}(k)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M\,,

given dually by the projection

f\longmapsto j^{k}_{p}f\,,

justify its interpretation as an infinitesimal neighborhood in
M
. The above somewhat tautological observations are summarized in the following commutative diagram

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
